#2877. Latin America has the copper and energy the AI build-out needs. Infrastructure is the test

Latin America’s copper, energy and mineral resources could play a growing role in supplying the global artificial-intelligence build-out, as data centers and electrification drive demand for power and raw materials.

But for investors, it's less about whether Latin America has the resources than whether governments can turn them into viable projects.

"We are going through an acceleration in energy demand growth. We need every possible energy source available," Michele Della Vigna, head of natural resources research for Goldman Sachs, told CNBC. "Ultimately, what drives economic growth is energy," he added.

Ben Kumar, head of strategy for wealth, investment and public policy at 7IM, suggested Latin America could benefit, given the physical materials needed for AI equipment and data centers. "We are currently neutral in the short term, but if the materials and mining cycle continues, we could see this moving to positive in a few months," he said.

The investment case varies across the region. Chile has copper; Argentina is expanding oil and gas exports; and Brazil has abundant renewable power and significant rare earth reserves.

Chile's copper sector illustrates both the supply potential and the constraints to expanding supply. Copper production fell 8.5% in the first nine months of 2026, according to the state copper commission Cochilco, as lower ore grades, maintenance, and operational disruptions constrained supply.

But demand for refined copper is set to grow 1.6% this year and 2% next year, according to the International Copper Study Group. Supply is struggling to keep pace with demand from expanding electricity grids.

Chile's copper commission has raised its 2027 price forecast to $5.95 a pound, from $5.10. Codelco, the state-owned copper producer, may also have scope to increase supply, including through proposed public-private partnerships.

Rafael Ch, partner and senior analyst for Latin America and emerging markets at Signum Global Advisors, sees demand continuing to grow. "As countries expand electricity grids, they need more copper regardless of which power source they choose," he said.

The scale of the opportunity will depend in part on how quickly energy demand grows. Goldman Sachs forecasts that global power demand for data centers will grow by 170% by 2030. The IEA projected in April that global data center electricity consumption would rise from 485 terawatt-hours in 2025 to 950 TWh in 2030.

The investment case also depends on whether the region can capture more value from the resources it produces. Latin America and the Caribbean refined only 26% of the copper they mined in 2025, according to the IEA.

"Cautious optimism is justified and there will be massive critical mineral demand," said Monica de Bolle, senior fellow at the Peterson Institute for International Economics.

"But improving refining capacity will be crucial to capture more of that value," she added.

Some projects have already secured financing. Chilean explorer Marimaca Copper Corp selected a preferred lender for a $400 million debt facility to build a mine on Oct. 28. The funding will go towards developing an open-pit mine in the north of Chile that will produce 50,000 metric tons of refined copper annually. Production is expected from 2029.

Argentina shows the other side of the opportunity: strong oil and gas resources, but insufficient infrastructure to get more production to global markets.

"I see an opportunity in the infrastructure that gets Argentine oil and gas to market. A producer in Vaca Muerta can have excellent wells and still struggle to expand exports without pipeline capacity and access to ports.

That gives investors a reason to look at transport and export infrastructure alongside production," Rafael Ch, partner and senior analyst for Latin America and emerging markets at Signum Global Advisors, added. Vaca Muerta is Argentina's vast shale basin.

Argentina is also attracting investment in drilling and export infrastructure. International capital markets have reopened to investment in drilling and pipelines in Argentina. That investment has already driven the build-out of a pipeline and port. Argentina will then be able to export far more crude oil. Chevron and American oil tycoon Harold Hamm have also increased their investments in Argentina.

Brazil's case rests partly on its renewable-heavy power mix. About 89% of the country's electricity was generated from renewable sources in 2025, according to the country's Ministry of Mines and Energy.

"That puts Brazil in a strong position to benefit as AI and data centers drive up demand," Stephanie Link, chief investment strategist and portfolio manager at Hightower Advisors, told CNBC.

But while Brazil has the world's second-largest reported rare-earth reserves, its estimated mine production is far smaller than China's. USGS estimates put Brazil's output at 2,000 metric tons in 2025, compared with China's 270,000 metric tons. Brazil's reserves are estimated at 11 million metric tons by the USGS, second only to China's 44 million metric tons.

For investors, the gap between Brazil's vast reserves and limited current production is both the opportunity and the execution risk.

For investors, the policy question is whether governments can make projects viable and keep them on track.

Luis Costa, Citi's global head of emerging markets strategy, said governments need to offer incentives, particularly in the early stages of projects, because infrastructure lags the region's resource wealth. He also stressed that support must last: infrastructure projects can take several years to develop.

The World Bank's Latin America and the Caribbean Economic Update for 2026 projects growth for the region of 2.2%, and highlights the "restricted fiscal space, sticky inflation and high borrowing costs."

"The economic policy and fiscal policy needs to get in line as markets are globally becoming more sensitive to fiscal stories," said Costa.

Brazil will need to establish fiscal credibility to attract investment, particularly if higher public spending puts additional pressure on debt and borrowing costs. "If investors doubt the government can stabilize public debt, companies' long-term financing costs could stay high even as the central bank lowers its policy rate," Ch added.

With Argentina set for national elections in October 2027, political continuity will also matter for energy investment. "A return to tighter currency controls or persistent deficits would make companies more likely to delay projects, even in an oil field with attractive economics," said Ch.

Pro-business President Javier Milei faces a tight race for re-election, though a change in leadership would not necessarily undermine medium- to long-term investment.

"There is also the possibility of a pragmatic Peronist ticket emerging, which markets would be satisfied with," said Nicolas Jaquier, emerging-market fixed-income portfolio manager at Ninety One.

For those governing across Latin America, policies to unlock its wealth of resources will be key for it to emerge as a winner from the AI demand boom.

"Supply chain security is becoming more and more relevant, and if commodities take off Latin America can be a clear and obvious winner," said Kumar.

#2876. Egor Demin’s standout outing leaves Nets wanting more

TORONTO — To say the Nets have had a disappointing start to the season would be an understatement.

But on Thanksgiving, the Nets can at least be happy about struggling Egor Dëmin having a bounce-back performance. Probably the best outing of his heretofore underwhelming campaign.

"The guys off the bench, Egor, Noah [Clowney], they were amazing," coach Jordi Fernández said after the Nets' slump-busting victory in Milwaukee.

The Nets snapped their five-game skid that had been tied for the worst in the NBA. And Dëmin showed promising signs of snapping out of his own not-unrelated malaise, coming into Friday's game in Toronto.

Perhaps most auspicious was the fact that it wasn't just a hot shooting showing, but an all-around night.

Part of Dëmin's allure as the eighth overall pick was his size, a 6-foot-9 guard who could not just space the floor but rebound and use his wingspan on defense.

That's happened all too infrequently so far. But it did Wednesday against the Bucks.

Dëmin finished with 15 points, four assists, three rebounds and a game-high plus-15. And despite being 0-for-5 from deep, Dëmin — who has struggled horribly inside the arc — was 5-for-6 from 2-point range. He even added feisty defense.

For a player too often faced with the question of "What can he do but shoot?" this was the answer. One that has to get repeated, starting against the Raptors.

Egor Dëmin, who scored 15 points in the Nets' win over the Bucks, drives past Ryan Rollins during the victory. AP

"Yes, he went back to the simple things and started with this physicality on defense," Fernández said. "They felt him the whole game, his length. Then he can rebound. He can be in the gaps. And then offensively, just simple.

"And I know he's capable of [hitting] every shot. Every 3 that he takes, I think it's going in. He was 0-for-5, but guess what? I think all his shots were great. So that's the way I want him to play: with energy, touching the paint, chasing the ball with his teammates, four assists. Proud of him, for sure."

Three games ago, Fernández had pulled Dëmin from the starting lineup for the first time since November of his rookie campaign.

Egor Dëmin rises up for a layup during the Nets' win over the Bucks in Milwaukee. Jeff Hanisch-Imagn Images

Dëmin went into Milwaukee having averaged 11.6 points on just 32.2 percent shooting over his prior five games.

And his woes were the worst on the other end of the court, posting a horrid 137.9 defensive rating during that skid. Dëmin was an aggregate minus-57, with Fernández admitting the Russian needed to be better in terms of his physicality.

Dëmin acknowledged a need to improve and did so against the Bucks. His defensive engagement helped spark a game-changing third-quarter spurt and helped the Nets run — his blown dunk notwithstanding.

"Before anything else, I've got to be present mentally for this team, especially because I'm trying to step in front as a leader role, and help Julius [Randle] out from that standpoint — from that kind of perspective — and help Mike [Porter Jr.] and older guys out with the leadership role," Dëmin said.

"So first off, I've got to be in my own mind out there present, and enjoy the game. And for me, it all came from the defensive side of the game. I was locked in with that, and everything else came to me naturally. Because we were getting stops and getting in transition — not counting that missed dunk — but we were getting wide-open opportunities that we were able to execute."

Dëmin had a block and a steal in the same game for the first time this season. He had three deflections and got in passing lanes.

And when Dëmin checked in with 5:16 left in the third and the Nets down 65-60, he helped ignite a 13-2 run. He had five points and two assists in just 1 ¹/₂ minutes, giving them the lead for good.

"Everybody was able to just put their minds together and not just focus on team defense but their own island, how we say 'guard your yard,' " Dëmin said. "That's where it came from."

The next goal is for Dëmin and the Nets not to lose it before Friday's game in Toronto.

#2872. Nvidia chip buyers told to rule out Chinese renters to purchase GPUs, sources say

Neoclouds are being told they won't be able to purchase Nvidia chips if any of the computing capacity will be rented by Chinese companies, sources told CNBC.

It comes after a new rule restricting remote access to artificial-intelligence semiconductors was announced by the Trump administration on Nov. 16.

Chip sellers communicated the messaging to neoclouds after the announcement, which seeks to close an export controls loophole allowing Chinese companies to rent access to Nvidia's most advanced chips when hosted in data centers overseas, two people familiar with the situation told CNBC. The people asked not to be named when discussing private information.

The interim final rule was announced by the Department of Commerce's Bureau of Industry and Security, or BIS, and came into effect on Nov. 19. Previously imposed export controls have effectively banned the sale of the most advanced Nvidia chips to Chinese companies and their import into China.

Prior to the new rule's announcement, Nvidia had said it would not sell its chips to customers renting more than 50% of the graphics processing units, or GPUs, to Chinese companies, CNBC reported.

The new rule impacts the full supply chain, from the companies selling Nvidia chips to the neoclouds who rent access to computing power. Neocloud stocks have come under renewed pressure lately as investors questioned the sustainability of the AI boom.

Nvidia and the U.S. Department of Commerce's Bureau of Industry and Security did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

The change has prompted Nvidia chip sellers to look for new buyers of the semiconductor giant's GPUs, CNBC reported on Wednesday, citing three people familiar with the matter. Two of those people said it appeared chip sellers were seeking to offload GPUs because they were originally for use in data centers through which Chinese companies would rent access.

U.S. Trade Representative Jamieson Greer told CNBC's "Squawk Box" on Wednesday that the new BIS rule was intended to close "loopholes."

"Some rules were issued by the Commerce Department last week to close off indirect access, either smuggling through places like Southeast Asia or remote access from data centers in third countries," he said.

U.S. lawmakers have been looking to clamp down on China's access to advanced compute.

Separately, Nvidia warned its customers that the proposed Remote Access Security Act — which has passed the House of Representatives but is yet to pass the Senate — would give the U.S. government broader authority to control access to cloud services, potentially exposing both parties to "legal action," sources previously told CNBC.

#2870. Game Preview: Knicks vs. Heat, November 27, 2026

Back in the 1990s, one way to preserve basketball games for the future was to record them on a VHS cassette. For all the younger folks, this was a big, chunky, rectangular thing that had magnetic tape on the inside. Whenever a game was on TV, you popped your cassette into the video recorder, pressed "REC," and from that point on, you could rewatch anything you recorded whenever you wanted. Or for as long as the tape held up, for that matter.

My favorite recorded event back then was Game Five of the first round of the playoffs in 1999 between your New York Knicks and the Miami Heat. It ended with Allan Houston's historic leaning jumper with 0.8 seconds to go, sending the top-seeded team coached by the Godfather of Schenectady packing and the underdog Bockers on an improbable run all the way to the NBA Finals. The tape wasn't just special to me because of the historic shot or the basketball played with bad intentions back then. It was also awesome because it showed the Knicks beating the Heat. Sorry, Grandma, I know you taught me not to hate anyone, but those were the Heat, my favorite team's biggest rival for years.

Now, before A'ja Wilson's boyfriend thinks he can hold a candle to the guy Patrick Ewing once offered one of his kidneys to, let me make it clear: That was a different time, a different rivalry. Whoever experienced it firsthand knows what I mean by that. When the Knicks meet the Heat on Friday night for their fourth NBA Cup game of the season, it will be different, much different. Different players, different coaches. Heck, it all seems like a different world. But make no mistake about it—it's still Knicks versus Heat. Which means the stakes are just a bit higher than in a game versus the magicians from the nation's capital or the royalty from "The Big Tomato."

Friday's game will be particularly interesting because it gives the Knicks a first glimpse at the newest version of the Heat, featuring Greece's national hero Giannis Antetokounmpo, who also happens to be one of the greatest and probably most unique basketball players this generation has seen. The project started pretty badly, with Giannis continuously reminding everyone who cared and everyone who didn't that he dearly missed Milwaukee, the city he had called home for every second of his storied NBA career until then. Then the schematic issues befell the not-so-hot Heat, who struggled with spacing, players playing in new roles, and the daunting task of implementing a headstrong player with immense gravity into a completely new system. Defense? Haha, good one!

But after a 3-6 start, the second-oldest team in the league decided this wasn't the way to ride off into the sunset. Or rather, their famous coach made plenty of adjustments. Erik Spoelstra, as we all know, is one of the best in the business. Not only at taking average rosters into the Play-In Tournament but indeed at coaching that sport with the orange leather. He inserted young Pelle Larsson into the starting lineup, found a spacing solution for the Heat, doubled down on their identity as a full-court, offensive-minded team, and then watched his guys reel off seven mostly impressive victories in a row (among them wins over Houston and Detroit) before falling to Philly on Wednesday night.

What can we expect from the Heat specifically? They love to get up and down the court and play at the third-highest pace in the entire league. Currently, only the Memphis Grizzlies score more points than Spoelstra's squad, and the Heat do it by shooting a high percentage from the field, drawing boatloads of fouls, and making use of two post players. Yes, you read that right—post players. Both Giannis and A'ja Wilson's boyfriend can effectively score with their backs to the basket, and despite many worries, they can actually coexist next to one another. One of them fills the dunker spot almost all the time, they rotate decisively in screen actions, and overall, Miami runs its sets fast and ferociously to get them rolling downhill. They don't just set screens to see where it leads them; they want to get into an action quickly before any lanes can close.

It works, partly because the players around Giannis and that guy who made a habit last year of disliking the team that won a championship before him are all doing a good job in their roles. Klay Thompson adds shooting (43.2 percent from downtown), Swedish Swiss Army knife Pelle Larsson provides a little bit of everything, and playmaker Davion Mitchell has all of a sudden turned into one of the better point guards in the league at age 28, averaging career highs of 17 points and 8.6 assists per contest while also shooting 51 percent from the floor and 43-plus percent from behind the arc. The bench has some older vets more or less collecting paychecks so far, but Bobby Portis and Andrew Wiggins clearly have some talent. Apparently, that also describes Ryan Conwell, a stocky second-round guard who has been a pleasant surprise for Miami.

That whole group will definitely look to waltz into Madison Square Garden on Friday with the idea of showing the champs that they are a team to be reckoned with. Whether they truly are remains to be seen; there are plenty of things that could uproot the positivity currently sprawling across South Beach. But the Knicks better be prepared for a tough test, and not because they might still be missing some of their injured guys or because they are coming off an emotionally draining victory against Cleveland on Wednesday. Not even because A'ja Wilson might have taught her boyfriend a few more tricks this offseason after winning her fifth WNBA MVP trophy.

No, it's going to be tough because it's Heat versus Knicks. And even if that doesn't mean what it did in the days of the VHS cassette, it still means something.

Who: Miami Heat (10-7) at New York Knicks (12-6)Date: Friday, November 27, 2026Time: 7:00 PM ETPlace: Madison Square Garden, New York, NYTV: Prime VideoFollow: @ptknicksblog and bsky

#2865. Spain’s Pablo Hernández de Cos set to lead European Central Bank

Pablo Hernández de Cos is set to succeed Christine Lagarde as president of the European Central Bank next year.

He received backing from euro zone leaders at the European Council Summit on Thursday. The role still requires formal appointment after consultation with the European Parliament and the ECB's Governing Council, but neither has veto power and the decision follows intense political wrangling to reach a consensus on who should serve the eight-year term.

De Cos is a 55-year-old economist and career central banker who rose through the ranks of the Bank of Spain, starting as a researcher in the 1990s and working in various departments before becoming governor from 2018 to June 2024.

He chaired the Basel Committee on Banking Supervision from 2019 to 2024, and has been General Manager of the Basel-based Bank for International Settlements since July 2025.

Both de Cos and his main rival for the ECB top job, the Netherlands' Klaas Knot, were widely seen as candidates with deep knowledge and experience in monetary policy and financial stability — attributes that took on particular importance at a time of resurgent inflation, fragmentation and political uncertainty.

Lagarde had extensive experience in financial institutions but was criticized by some early in her tenure for a lack of central banking experience.

De Cos gained sufficient political backing in closed-door discussions and negotiations since Lagarde said she would step down on Feb. 7, 2027, over seven years after she became the first woman to helm the ECB. That was despite a final push by the Netherlands at the European Council meeting this week backing Knot.

Representatives from the euro area's biggest economies — Germany, France, Italy, Spain and the Netherlands — have been horse-trading as they look to secure representation on the central bank's six-member Executive Board.

Influential roles as chief economist, expected to go to Germany's Markus Brunnermeier, and head of the Markets Portfolio, for which France's Laurence Boone has reportedly been tapped, also change hands in 2027.

Analysts say de Cos's commentary and actions over more than two decades at Spain's central bank paint him as moderately dovish — inclined to favor lower interest rates to support economic growth — but also as a pragmatist who has been willing to adapt.

That has included support for strong rate hikes during the energy price shock that followed Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022, but also giving staunch backing for rates below zero and the huge stimulus package launched by then-ECB President Mario Draghi to reboot the euro zone economy in 2019.

De Cos quipped in 2024 that he was an "owl" after being asked if he was a hawk or a dove, emphasizing his focus on data and the situation at hand.

"It is true that Hernández de Cos supported the ECB's uber-dovish monetary policy during the period when inflation was persistently below target. However, his monetary policy views may well be regime-dependent," Christian Kopf, head of fixed income and foreign exchange at Union Investment, said in emailed comments.

"We are now clearly in a higher-inflation regime, with core inflation in the euro area expected to remain above the ECB's target through 2029."

Kopf added that de Cos was the candidate with the "strongest technical understanding of monetary policy and central banking," which he said was important at a time when the world is "witnessing an assault on central bank independence, not only in the United States but also in countries such as Japan."

"By appointing a career technocrat such as Hernández de Cos as ECB President, the European Council would send a strong signal that Europe will not waver in its commitment to price stability and that the euro will remain a hard currency," Kopf said.

Spain's de Cos as president, Germany's Brunnermeier as chief economist and France's Boone as head of market operations represent a "carefully crafted package deal," Kopf also noted, because it balances representation from northern and southern European countries. The result was partially due to the knock-on effects of the German government pushing hard for the chief economist position, he said.

Along with a desire among politicians for influence, a north-south balance is seen as important as the northern countries are seen as leaning more hawkish — more inclined to opt for rate hikes and restrictive policy to tame inflation — while southern are perceived as more dovish.

John Orchard, chairman of the Digital Monetary Institute at think tank OMFIF, said that as a "central banker's central banker," de Cos had "managed to earn respect from typically hawkish northern observers."

Another supportive factor of de Cos's candidacy was the stellar growth rate of many of those southern economies in recent years.

Once the "sick men" of the euro area's 2010s sovereign debt crisis, Portugal, Italy, Spain and Greece have become some of the bloc's strongest growth drivers in recent years.

Factors such as strong tourism, relative political stability, increased foreign investment and strengthening public finances have boosted southern economies. Meanwhile, the euro zone's traditional powerhouse Germany has stuttered amid soaring industrial costs and a struggling autos market.

Orchard said that some believed that Lagarde's presidency had ushered in an "esprit de corps" at the ECB, with policymakers focused on the interests of the whole European economy.

That would, in theory, reduce the importance of nationality among leadership and of traditional notions of hawkish northern nations and dovish southern ones. However, Orchard also noted the recurrent theme of national representation in the recent debate and the perception of Knot as a hawkish choice.

Diverging performance is a core challenge for any leader of the ECB, which must set monetary policy for 21 economies all growing at different speeds and with their own domestic priorities and inflationary pressures.

But it has become particularly pressing this year as the bloc's second-biggest economy, France, is teetering on the edge of its own potential debt crisis.

Moves in French markets and whether the ECB needs to take more extreme measures beyond continuing to urge fiscal discipline will remain in focus for de Cos.

The ECB Executive Board's view of what measures could be taken to prevent market disorder and under what conditions was a key topic under discussion when choosing who should fill the roles, according to insiders.

Despite political efforts to pass a budget that will reduce its budget deficit to around 5% of GDP, investors have been driving up French borrowing costs on concerns over its mounting public debt-to-GDP ratio, high budget deficit and contracting economy.

Risks are being compounded by political uncertainty ahead of France's presidential election in April, which is stymying business activity and raising wider questions about EU unity as far-right and far-left candidates pull ahead.

Rising French yields and the widening spread in France's borrowing costs over Germany's have led to speculation over whether the ECB might need to deploy tools such as liquidity injections or its never-used Transmission Protection Instrument to protect other countries from contagion and support the euro.

This would be politically controversial unless France agreed to stringent fiscal discipline measures akin to those that southern nations signed up to during the Sovereign Debt Crisis — which could be complicated if far-right presidential frontrunner Marine Le Pen, who has a combative relationship with EU institutions, wins.

Ken Egan, head of European sovereigns at ratings agency KBRA, said fragmentation was always a challenge for the ECB, which does not want to see divergent financing conditions and high sovereign spreads across the bloc.

However, he said that in the case of France he believed we were "some way from having a serious conversation about ECB intervention."

"If anything, sovereign spreads have pulled back a bit and tightened, and some measures of market liquidity have also improved in recent weeks," he said by email.

"That doesn't mean the underlying risks have disappeared, but I think it's important to distinguish between periods of market volatility and a more fundamental breakdown in monetary policy transmission."

De Cos also takes up the mantle as Europe faces yet another surge in price pressures.

Euro area inflation rose to 4% in October, its highest level in more than three years. Energy was the biggest contributor, as the Iran war drives up oil and gas prices and tightens the supply of refined fuels.

That leaves the central bank facing a difficult policy balance, preventing higher energy costs from spreading into wages and wider prices while avoiding unnecessary damage to growth.

De Cos will inherit major supranational projects, including plans for a digital euro and Europe's long-delayed capital-markets integration. In a recent speech, Lagarde warned that each year without capital markets integration meant "more of our savings are invested abroad and more of our growing firms look elsewhere for funding."

France is not the only euro area country heading for a pivotal election next year, with votes in Finland, Greece, Italy, Estonia, Slovakia and Slovenia also expected, adding to the politically uncertain climate.

It is also not the only country grappling with higher government borrowing costs, public debt and a struggle to find fiscal headroom, a growing problem among many developed countries.

The ECB's Financial Stability Review meanwhile warned this month of mounting risks from European investors' growing exposure to the U.S. economy, and equity and credit markets' vulnerability to AI.

Outgoing head Lagarde, meanwhile, told the European Banking Congress in Frankfurt this month that her biggest concern was now cyber threats, an area she said policymakers had not paid enough attention.

"The one [worry] that keeps me awake, and many of you awake ​as well ... ​is ⁠AI applied together with cyber threat," she said.

#2862. Knicks’ win streak doesn’t stop Mike Brown from being blunt about team: ‘We haven’t arrived’

Mike Brown isn't letting his Knicks team get comfortable.

They have responded emphatically to their shaky start to the season — after losing their first three games of the year, the Knicks have gone 12-3. They have won six in a row and are tied for second in the Eastern Conference ahead of their game against Giannis Antetokounmpo's Heat Friday night at Madison Square Garden.

They are starting to resemble the team that finished last year with a championship. But Brown holds the same sentiment he did during those first few games.

Mike Brown reacts on the sidelines during the Knicks' win over the Cavaliers on Nov. 25, 2026, at Madison Square Garden. Jason Szenes for New York Post

Just like if you're down, it's not the end of the world, you're that close to flipping it. You try to be even-keeled."

Friday's game is the Knicks' fourth and final NBA Cup group-stage game. And they face an uphill battle to defend their title.

#2861. Even in a lost Eagles season, greatness can happen

I'm going to tell you something you already know.

The Philadelphia Eagles are not going to win the Super Bowl this year. In fact, they are almost certainly not going to make the playoffs, even though their epic 39-38 back-from-the-dead victory over the Cowboys in Dallas on Thanksgiving afternoon increased their odds of doing so just a tad.

We've known for more than a month that this was a lost season. When Jalen Hurts was lost for the year with blood clots, Lane Johnson retired, half the team got injured and the front office traded away Cam Jurgens and Jordan Davis, it became obvious the franchise was punting on 2026 and looking to the future.

After winning their second Super Bowl two years ago and reaching the postseason five straight seasons under Nick Sirianni, that's OK. Lost seasons happen.

Not every season has to be about winning the Super Bowl. I know that's easier to say as fans of a franchise that now has two of them in the last decade, but it's the truth. Fans of prior generations who had to live through not just lost seasons, but lost decades, had to learn how to unearth greatness while trudging through the muck of a depressing campaign.

The Eagles' scintillating victory over the Cowboys yesterday was just such a moment. It probably won't result in the team making the playoffs, but it is a victory that will be remembered by every fan of this team for the rest of their lives.

Their rally from 21 points down is the largest deficit overcome in NFL history on Thanksgiving, and they did it against their most hated rival, in their building. They did it with incredible plays from the unlikeliest, and likeliest, corners of the roster.

Consider Joe Milton, III, the Eagles' fourth quarterback of the season, who began 2026 as Dallas' third-stringer and was signed by Howie Roseman off their practice squad back in October. After being thrust into the starting job following Tanner McKee's struggles as the back-up, Milton has thrived in the team's two games after the bye.

On Thursday afternoon in Dallas, he was electric.

Milton finished 21-of-31 passing for 331 yards, 3 TDs, one interception, and one rushing score on a 15 yard scramble to the end zone. He's making quite a case to be considered the team's back-up QB once Hurts is back next year.

His favorite target on the day, DeVonta Smith, had the game of his life. Connecting on multiple deep hook-ups against an awful Dallas secondary, Smith caught 11 balls on 14 targets for a career-high 200 yards and two TDs. It's unclear if he'll be available for the Eagles' next game in Arizona after being landing hard on his tailbone late in the 4th quarter of the Birds' comeback, but his efforts on Thursday were nothing short of heroic.

Ty Robinson, a name no one could have been expected to utter much heading into the game, was responsible for two of the afternoon's biggest plays. Trailing 31-17 early in the 4th quarter, Robinson's blocked punt allowed Kenny Moore to pick the ball up inside the five yard line and waltz into the end zone for a momentum-shifting score.

And then, with less than a minute to play and Dallas going for a game-sealing 4th-and-1 conversion, Robinson (with the help of Quinyon Mitchell's terrific defense on Cee Dee Lamb denying a slant pattern that was Dak Prescott's first option) sacked Prescott at midfield to put the Eagles a mere 10 yards out of field goal range.

Robinson finished with three QB pressures, two hurries, the aforementioned sack and blocked punt. The trade of Jordan Davis earlier this month has allowed Robinson to get more playing time, and he's taking advantage of it.

And how about Jake Elliott? After struggling over the last two seasons, especially from long distance (5-for-15 from beyond 50 yards), he has suddenly re-discovered his deep stroke and is enjoying a late-career renaissance. Overall, he's 13-for-14 this season, including a perfect 5-for-5 from beyond 50.

If you haven't heard the Dallas radio call of Elliott's 60-yarder yesterday, as well as the final two seconds of the game, enjoy this.

There were other performances worth remembering, too. Will Shipley stepped up and rushed for 46 yards on 10 carries after Saquon Barkley left the game with a stinger. Makai Lemon hauled in five balls for 51 yards, including a terrific 24-yard touchdown catch in the 4th quarter. Elijah Moore caught three balls for 57 yards. Jeremiah Trotter, Jr. led all players with 12 tackles (no other player on either team had more than nine, Byron Young). And Braden Mann continued pounding punts into the stratosphere, consistently helping the Eagles with field position whenever possible.

And kudos must be given to the much-maligned head coach and offensive coordinator. After failing to score more than 24 points in a single game this year, the offense put up 22 in the 4th quarter alone and 39 overall. Yes, it was against a porous Dallas defense, but we've seen Sean Mannion's offense struggle against the worst defenses in the league, including this very same Cowboys squad in late October when they lost 38-21 at the Linc.

I didn't love Sirianni's histrionics after the game. Stop jawing with fans, get yourself under control, and go give the opposing head coach a proper post-game handshake. Also, the decision to go for two points after drawing to 38-30 made no sense to me, either. But all that being said, Sirianni's players never folded the tents after getting behind 31-10 in the second half, and credit must be giving to the head coach for that.

Eagles fans haven't had to deal with many lost seasons over the last quarter century. Thankfully, we're not used to this. So it's easy to forget that, even in a season where nothing has gone your way, a legendary moment can be birthed from its ashes.

Sometimes, sports is just about moments and memories. And while there likely won't be a banner raised at Lincoln Financial Field at the beginning of next year, The Joe Milton Thanksgiving Game of 2026 will be remembered as an Eagles classic forever.

And that is something to be thankful for.

#2860. Trump, NATO chief caution Russia as attacks on Ukraine’s energy infrastructure intensify

U.S. President Donald Trump and NATO chief Mark Rutte issued a joint statement on Thursday urging Russia not to take any actions, intentionally or otherwise, that would further expand the Ukraine war.

The statement comes as Ukraine's allies sound the alarm over Russia's growing threat to European security and just one week after Trump's historic trilateral meeting with Russian President Vladimir Putin and Chinese President Xi Jinping in Asia.

Trump and Rutte described their meeting at the Mar-a-Lago club in Palm Beach, Florida on Wednesday as "very impactful," with Russia's nearly five-year-long war in Ukraine and the Iran war among the key topics of conversation.

"We also discussed the importance of Russia avoiding any actions that intentionally, or unintentionally, expand the war, and the importance of obtaining a peaceful resolution to the war as rapidly as possible," Trump and Rutte said in their statement.

Moreover, the two discussed the importance of further increasing defense spending as well as investment in the defense industrial base in both the U.S. and NATO countries, according to the statement.

Trump also said in a post on Truth Social Thursday that he "briefed NATO on the tremendous progress" being made in growing defense production, noting that the focus will be on building Patriot air defense systems, THAAD systems and Tomahawk cruise missiles, as well as "many new Weapons, both Offensive and Defensive, that have not yet been viewed by the Public, or other Countries."

"There has never been a time when the United States has produced more Military Equipment or Munitions than now. Our 'cupboard' will soon be full and, when it is, our Allies and Partners throughout the World will be benefitting greatly," he said in the post.

Trump's meeting with Xi and Putin, in which he described the Russian president as his "friend," sparked alarm among European lawmakers about Washington's commitment to transatlantic security.

NATO's Rutte, a seasoned diplomat known as a consensus builder during his nearly 14 years as Dutch prime minister, has repeatedly sought to de-escalate tensions in the alliance and frequently used flattery to win over the U.S. president.

While some have questioned whether this approach has delivered any tangible benefits, Trump has previously described Rutte as a "great leader" and NATO's "biggest asset."

Russia has stepped up its attacks on Ukraine's energy infrastructure in recent weeks, with further attacks on the central Ukrainian city of Kryvyi Rih overnight disrupting power supplies and bringing municipal electric public transport to a standstill, according to Oleksandr Vilkul, head of Kryvyi ​Rih's defense council.

Ukrainian officials on Wednesday said Russian attacks on the country's power sector had damaged energy infrastructure and caused power outages in five regions as cold winter weather sets in.

German Chancellor Friedrich Merz on Wednesday accused the Kremlin of "escalating" threats toward European security, citing actions in the Baltic region, Poland and on the Black Sea, and adding that "Russia knows exactly what it wants. And Russia wants more than Ukraine."

A spokesperson for Russia's Foreign Ministry did not immediately respond to a CNBC request for comment on Thursday morning. The Kremlin has repeatedly denied seeking military confrontation with NATO countries.

#2859. Judge blocks FCC early-renewal process for Disney-owned ABC stations, for now

A judge has temporarily blocked the Federal Communications Commission from continuing its early renewal of a set of broadcast station licenses for Disney-owned ABC.

Judge Loren L. AliKhan of U.S. District Court in Washington granted Disney the preliminary injunction it sought and denied the FCC's attempt to dismiss the suit.

"This is not a close case," wrote the federal judge. "Given the extreme, indeed existential, nature of the possible penalty Plaintiffs face—loss of their broadcast licenses—the court concludes that early license renewal proceedings create a risk sufficient to chill a broadcaster of ordinary firmness."

The broadcast network had filed the lawsuit and followed the FCC beginning its early review of the ABC licenses. The government agency cited concerns around ABC's diversity, equity and inclusion efforts as the reason behind the start of the early renewal — years ahead of their scheduled expiration.

"Today's decision is an important victory for the First Amendment," said an ABC spokesperson.

An FCC spokesperson said in a statement that the part of the decision granting ABC's motion "is wrong on the facts and wrong on the law," the Associated Press reported.

In her ruling, AliKhan said that early license renewal proceedings came over a year into the FCC's enforcement push against ABC, starting with an investigation into Disney's employment practices in March 2025 and leading to the renewal proceedings in April this year.

ABC on Tuesday had said that it continued to "face irreparable harm" from the administration, which "has been acting" through the FCC, which is run by President Donald Trump appointee Brendan Carr.

AliKhan, a Joe Biden appointee, noted in her ruling that ABC had to change how it handled guest appearances for top shows "The View" and "Jimmy Kimmel Live!" with no candidate for public office appearing on the former and a senatorial candidate's appearance on the latter uploaded to YouTube instead of being broadcast.

"The prospect of license non-renewal has also factored into plaintiffs' calculus of when and how they cover President Trump and political topics," said the judge.

ABC had alleged that Trump and Carr have been making comments "intended to chill their protected speech," and that such comments have escalated since the 2024 presidential election.

The White House and the FCC didn't immediately respond to requests for comment from CNBC.

— CNBC's Dan Mangan contributed to the story

#2858. Lakers vs. Spurs Preview: A big first test

After nearly a full week off, the Lakers (15-3) have their first true, big test of the season against the Spurs (15-4) on Friday. The two teams will meet three times this year, though not again until February after this duel.

Who: Los Angeles Lakers at San Antonio Spurs

For more than a week now, this matchup has loomed over the Lakers' schedule. Each win during their recent homestand meant more and more of an eye turned toward this contest.

The Spurs stand alone as the measuring stick in the Western Conference after the Thunder were decimated by injury. It means every team is going to give them their best shot with the Lakers the latest team loading up for their chance at a defining win.

And while the Lakers have had the better part of a week to prepare, the Spurs were already on the receiving end of one of those haymakers on Wednesday. In an NBA Cup match that impacted LA's chances of advancing, the Blazers knocked off the Spurs, dropping them into a four-way tie atop the group with every non-Kings team sitting at 2-1.

The Spurs losing has been a rarity since the opening week of the season. After dropping their first two contests to the Thunder and Rockets, San Antonio won 12 straight games and 15 of 16 games prior to Wednesday's defeat.

Victor Wembanyama is, obviously, at the center of that. Through 18 games, Wemby is averaging 30.7 points, 12.5 rebounds, 3.2 assists and a staggering 3.8 blocks per game. He's doing it on 54.3% shooting, a career-best mark despite shooting just 31.7% from three, a career-worst rate.

Perhaps unsurprisingly, they sport the best net rating in the league with the fourth-best offensive rating and third-best defensive rating. Their damage comes in the paint and at the rim where they rank top five in points in the paint per game (second), rim field goal makes (third), attempts (fifth) and percentage (fifth) and overall paint field goal percentage (third).

Their three-guard approach of De'Aaron Fox, Dylan Harper and Stephon Castle around Wemby allows them to relentlessly attack the rim at the cost of shooting, where they are 21st in 3-point rate and 28th in 3-point percentage.

In short, it will be a long night for Walker Kessler as a rim protector.

The good news is that all signs point to an Austin Reaves return. His absence will span more than two weeks, but he was a participant in practice on Wednesday and spoke with the media after, which is typically a giveaway that a player will return from injury.

After watching Luka Dončić run on empty in the final games of that aforementioned homestand, Reaves' return couldn't come at a better time. The rust will need to be kicked off and it's unrealistic to expect Reaves to continue his All-Star-level play out of the gate, but having him available is a huge boost.

It also gives the team a full picture look at how they stack up against the Spurs. While this is still just one game and there are still 60+ remaining on the schedule after Friday, this is a big one and both teams will treat it as such.

Reaves is officially listed as questionable for the game. It's the first time he hasn't been listed as out since suffering the injury.

San Antonio could be without two of their guards. De'Aaron Fox hasn't played in the last two contests and is questionable with a shin contusion. Dylan Harper, who has not played in the last four games, has been upgraded to questionable with a hiop external rotator strain. Tobias Harris (calf strain) is out.

#2857. Steelers fans spare no one amid Aaron Rodgers, Mike McCarthy feud

PITTSBURGH – Steelers fans expressed broad disgust with the state of the franchise in the hours before their team's Black Friday matchup with the Denver Broncos on Nov. 27 at Acrisure Stadium.

Aaron Rodgers' decision to air out his grievances with the coaching in an explosive interview on ESPN's "Pat McAfee Show" on Wednesday, Nov. 25 didn't sit well with many.

That didn't mean they were taking the side of coach Mike McCarthy either, though. Most seemed to believe Rodgers' assertions that the first-year coach was falling asleep in meetings, missing them entirely and failing to conduct basic film study.

Required reading: Aaron Rodgers goes scorched earth on Mike McCarthy after Steelers benching

"It's wild, man," Mark Hirschfeld said as fans gathered outside Acrisure Stadium ahead of the game. "I've just been trying to stay awake, unlike Mike McCarthy.

"People are mad, and I don't think they're mad only at Aaron Rodgers, I think they're mad at the organization as a whole. People don't like that the Steelers are a laughingstock."

The weather wasn't helping the mood. It was a raw, gray and soggy November morning in the North Shore parking lots, which were filling lazily on the morning after the Thanksgiving holiday.

Tailgates were sparse beyond some hardcore zones. And many fans were reluctant to talk to USA TODAY about the state of the Steelers franchise.

Call it a citywide hangover from 48-or-so hours of chaos that began late Tuesday, when the Steelers announced they were benching Rodgers, making him inactive and starting longtime backup Mason Rudolph for a crucial game against the 8-2 Broncos.

Rodgers responded by appearing with McAfee to rhetorically torch the organization, asserting that general manager Omar Khan and owner Art Rooney II were aware of McCarthy's alleged shortcomings and chose not to act on them by granting his preferred changes to the play calling structure. For those reasons, few were feeling sympathetic toward the front office.

"When you sign a guy like Aaron Rodgers, you are signing up for this show," Hirschfeld said. "I don't think anybody thought it would end any other way besides wild success or some short of giant show like this."

Apparently, Rooney did. He told the Pittsburgh Post-Gazette on Thursday that, because of his positive experience with Rodgers in 2025, he wasn't expecting things to go off the proverbial rails in the way they did.

Rodgers led the team to a division title a year ago. He did not play well in a playoff loss to Houston in January, but there was belief that he was the right quarterback to start the McCarthy era after he was hired to replace Mike Tomlin, because of their more than a decade of previous success together with the Green Bay Packers.

Matt Spielvogle's feelings were mixed about who was most in the wrong.

"Whenever you have a head coach actually doing this, if it's all true, the public deserves to know," the Altoona, Pa., native said. "We're paying $500 a ticket, I think the fans deserve to know what's going on inside the locker room.

"I agree with a lot of things that [Rodgers] is doing, but I also disagree with a lot of things that he's doing. I understand the whole secrecy. You can't take it outside. I played college football. I get it. But at the same time, how long is it going to go on? There's a reason why we're failing."

The awkward reality is that the Steelers haven't failed yet. Pittsburgh entered Week 12 at 6-4 and was the seventh seed in the AFC playoff race without a ton of pressure coming from other teams behind them.

The two-game losing streak that prompted the furor with Rodgers followed a four-game winning streak that had fans dreaming of the franchise's first playoff win since 2016.

The offense had shown gradual improvement and defensive coordinator Patrick Graham's soft zone coverage scheme had helped the highly-paid defense led by Cam Heyward and T.J. Watt regain some footing compared to last season.

NFL discourse tends to exist in the present tense, though. When asked whether McCarthy should remain in place as coach beyond this season, most fans demurred or expressed a desire for the franchise to move on.

"Personally, I think let him finish out the year, then fire him," fan Santino Fischio said just outside the North Shore's Kamin Science Center, adjacent to Acrisure Stadium. "But do I think we're going to do that? No."

In fact, Rooney told the Post-Gazette that he has "100% confidence" in the coach, despite Rodgers' assertions that he had largely lost the locker room with his conduct.

"I figured that's how they were going to do it," Fischio said. "I feel like they were kind of using [Rodgers] like a scapegoat."

Rennes must now find a permanent successor as they look to revive a season that has quickly gone off the rails.

#2855. Switzerland’s biggest bank set for showdown with lawmakers as capital fight intensifies amid crisis planning

UBS's crisis planning has won qualified support from market regulators, which called its plan "executable" in principle. But Switzerland's biggest bank still faces a major fight over sweeping plans that would force it to hold more capital against its foreign subsidiaries.

FINMA, the Swiss markets regulator, said Wednesday that UBS's resolvability — or its ability to fail safely, without wreaking havoc on the economy — has improved since it integrated rival Credit Suisse, with progress made in implementing the emergency plan across group-wide resolution planning.

The preferred resolution strategy — involving plans to allow for recapitalization and restructuring through the use of loss-absorbing bail-in bonds — is "executable," FINMA said. The regulator formally approved UBS's recovery plan for the first time since its ​Credit Suisse integration.

How UBS would withstand a future crisis remains in sharp focus among Swiss authorities ever since the lender's emergency takeover of stricken rival Credit Suisse in 2023.

UBS is now the country's only global systemically important bank, and the Swiss government wants it to hold stronger capital cushions, specifically in relation to its foreign subsidiaries, which would help it withstand another banking collapse.

UBS has pushed back against the overhaul, saying the measures are disproportionate and would damage its international competitiveness, forcing it to hold an extra $16 billion in capital.

A parliamentary showdown now looms, with the National Council, Switzerland's lower house, scheduled to debate the proposal Dec. 16.

On Tuesday, the lower house's economics committee backed plans to force the bank to support its foreign subsidiaries with 90% high-quality core Common Equity Tier 1, or CET1, capital. The Council of States, the country's upper house, approved the 90% plan in September.

The Swiss government had earlier proposed a 100% threshold. UBS wants it to be no more than 50%.

UBS CEO Sergio Ermotti told CNBC in an interview Oct. 28 that 90% would not allow the company to be a "competitive player" long-term. But he would not be drawn on speculation that the measures could push the bank to relocate out of Switzerland.

In a statement, the bank said the economics committee's recommendation would herald further excessive tightening of Swiss capital requirements, "which are already among the most stringent globally."

FINMA said in its Nov. 25 update that the goal is to avoid government solvency support and emergency law.

"The aim is to enable the authorities, in the event of a crisis, to choose the option that offers the best prospects of success in safeguarding financial stability and ensuring the continuation of systemically important functions," the regulator said.

#2854. Nvidia has lost $2 trillion in value. Here's what options traders expect from earnings

Jensen Huang's forced exit from Nvidia shocked Wall Street and Washington, wiped $2 trillion off its market value, and raised serious questions about national security and regulation of the AI complex. Traders, for the moment, must content themselves with the comparatively mundane business of Tuesday's earnings, and options traders are expecting big moves.

With volatility in the stock near the highest since April 2025, options traders are now pricing a 7% reaction in the stock after its report, the biggest implied move going into an Nvidia report since February 2025, and well above the median move of 3.2% over the last eight quarters.

Implied volatility reached 68 Monday, compared to under 40 in early October, according to ThinkOrSwim data. Nvidia vol reached a multi-year high above 92 in the aftermath of President Trump's tariff Liberation day in April '25. In other words, the company that for much of the AI boom was the most reliable winner in the market, is now more volatile than all but 35 companies in the S&P 500.

"Longer term, I see significant downside risk unless Nvidia can attract an exceptional leader, someone like Tim Cook or AMD's Lisa Su," Nigam Arora, founder of the Arora report, said in a text ahead of the company's after-market report on Tuesday.

Since hitting all-time highs in October, Nvidia shares have fallen 35%, shedding $2 trillion in market capitalization along the way. Including Nvidia, there are only five companies in the S&P 500 worth more than $2 trillion. However, there is evidence that bulls have been holding out hope while bears snatch up profits: the ratio of open puts to call contracts has actually widened towards calls, according to Barchart data. The ratio is currently 0.87, down from a one-year high of 1.02 two weeks ago and the lowest since the first week of October.

The most popular contract by volume on Monday was the 155-strike put expiring at the bell Monday, but the most popular trade for later expiry was the 160-strike call expiring Thursday, and the second-most-common purchase was the 190-strike call expiring Friday, SpotGamma data show.

One catch is that most call contracts traded Monday looked like neutral to bearish positions opened by traders selling calls. Of the 5 million options traded on Monday, 3 million were calls, but more were likely opened by sellers than buyers. Of the $2.3 billion in options premium traded on Nvidia Monday, $1.2 billion was tied to puts, SpotGamma data show.

With bears taking profits in puts the past month, there's another potential snag on open interest. There are very few open put contracts at or below the $157 level at which the stock closed Monday. That suggests any bad surprise would find many traders with little downside protection.

By the same token, there's notable open interest in call contracts from the 175-strike and above, suggesting profit-taking on a bounce could limit Nvidia's recovery past levels it breached over the past week.

Either way, Tuesday's earnings could provide fireworks.

#2853. Game Preview: San Antonio Spurs vs Los Angeles Lakers

Luka Doncic and the Los Angeles Lakers have kicked off the post-LeBron era of the Purple and Gold with a bang. They currently boast the league's best record, an achievement built upon the offensive prowess of Doncic and an opportunistic defense that's been the best at generating turnovers that lead to buckets. The caveat to all this though is that, much like San Antonio, they haven't really had to play anyone yet, as is demonstrated by their opponents holding a cumulative .384 winning percentage, the lowest mark in the league thus far. Doncic has also been carrying the sort of offensive load throughout the first quarter of the season that is typically unsustainable across 82 games. There's a strong possibility that he gets some reinforcements tonight in the form of Austin Reaves, who is hoping to make his return tonight from a bruised heel that's kept him out of 5 consecutive contests and 6 of Los Angeles' last 7.

The Spurs have had a great start to the year overall, but similar to the Lakers there's been a bit of chatter about the quality of their opponents in their 15 wins. While taking care of business against teams you should beat is a sign of being a good team, San Antonio is hoping to be a great team. Even coming off a loss to a Portland team that they faced in the playoffs last season, this game against the Lakers and a player of Doncic's caliber will be their stiffest test since the loss last week to Cade Cunningham and the Detroit Pistons, the night Dylan Harper went down with his injury. With both Harper and De'Aaron Fox both possibly back in the lineup, can they quiet some of that noise tonight?

San Antonio Spurs (15-4) vs Los Angeles Lakers (15-3)November 27 2026 | 8:30 PM CTWatch: Amazon Prime | Listen: WOAI (1200 AM)Line: San Antonio -5.5

Spurs Injuries: Tobias Harris, calf (OUT), Dylan Harper, hip (questionable), De'Aaron Fox, shin (questionable)

Lakers Injuries: Austin Reaves, heel (questionable)

Luka Doncic is having an absurd start to the season, leading the NBA with a scoring average of 36.6 points per game. He has an insane 40% usage rate though, amongst the highest in NBA history were it to stay at that level all season and very much reminiscent of how his days with the Dallas Mavericks went. Asking Doncic, who has only played 70 or more games twice in his career, to keep this up for an entire season seems like a tall task, but Doncic hasn't really shown a willingness to play any other way. San Antonio does have more than a few options to throw at him though out on the perimeter, plus the benefit of the always-lurking Victor Wembanyama to deter his drives to the paint. Doncic is capable of beating a lot of teams by himself, but if San Antonio can minimize some of the damage that the rest of the Lakers are capable of playing off of him, they could have a recipe for success. A weak point for him: he's averaging 5.1 turnovers per game, which leads the league.

While the Lakers have been the best team at forcing turnovers this season, San Antonio has been among the best at taking care of ball. Los Angeles forces 18.8 turnovers a game and scores 24 points per game off of them, fueling their already lethal offense with easy opportunities in transition. With Dylan Harper and DeAaron Fox both questionable for tonight's game, the Silver and Black could find themselves lacking in strong ball handlers that can protect the rock and set the offense up in the halfcourt. Stephon Castle, who has kept his turnovers down this year in general and especially as of late with his fellow two guards missing some time, will need to play clean basketball tonight and limit some of those easy looks that this Lakers team has thrived on.

Dylan Harper was upgraded to questionable late last night on San Antonio's injury report. Tonight would be just 11 days since he suffered his injury against the Pistons, which would put him way ahead of the 3-week timetable that was initially announced after the injury. The Spurs very much need everything Harper has to offer when healthy if they hope to compete with the league's best, but how limited he will be in his first game back remains to be seen. It wouldn't be at all shocking though to see Mitch Johnson and his staff adhere to some sort of a minutes restriction with the 20 year old Harper, but the fact that he's healthy enough to be considered questionable for a game already is a great sign that he'll be out there sooner rather than later.

#2850. Private markets are more accessible than ever for investors. Getting out is another matter

Retail investors are lining up to back private funds. But education on how restrictive these funds can be, particularly on when and how much money they can get back, may not be keeping pace with the influx of new capital.

PwC research has found that 77% of fund distributors surveyed said access to private markets is becoming important or very important to what they plan to offer clients, but just 15% of asset managers said they provide comprehensive education for retail investors.

Unlike the public markets retail investors are more used to, some private-market vehicles limit when investors can get their money back to set repurchase windows, and even then may return only part of what they request.

A spike in redemption requests at several private-credit funds earlier this year exposed the tension between giving investors regular opportunities to cash out and holding assets that can be difficult to sell quickly.

Blackstone restricted withdrawals from its $79 billion flagship nontraded BCRED private credit fund to 5% of shares in June, after redemption requests hit 10% during the second quarter. Apollo also capped withdrawals in its Apollo Debt Solutions vehicle at 5% after second-quarter redemption requests surged to almost 17%. Blue Owl, Morgan Stanley and Partners Group also limited investor withdrawals from some of their funds.

Limitations on redemptions are a key trade-off in structures such as LTAFs in the U.K., ELTIFs in the EU and interval funds in the U.S., which broaden access to less-liquid assets.

It's emerged as a major pressure point as those structures come under closer scrutiny.

"Private markets can have very different characteristics from the products many wealth clients are used to, particularly around liquidity, valuations and holding periods," said Albertha Charles, global asset and wealth management leader at PwC.

"Investors and advisers need to understand how these products work, including their liquidity and valuation characteristics, so they can make informed decisions about whether they are appropriate."

PwC's 2026 Asset Management Revolution report, published Nov. 23, surveyed 250 asset managers, 100 institutional investors and 100 distributors across 36 countries and regions. More than half of respondents represented firms with over $20 billion in assets under management.

Among the study's key findings, education and liquidity are seen as the main hurdles as private markets reach a broader investor base. Distributors said better advisor and client education would help expand access, while liquidity, lock-ups and suitability requirements remain major barriers. Private equity and private credit were also the areas where distributors saw some of the biggest gaps in advisor knowledge.

Private credit appears likely to draw the strongest demand — two-thirds (66%) of distributors expect it to lead client interest in private markets, compared with 46% for private equity.

Regulators in several major markets are creating structures to widen access to long-term and illiquid assets. The U.K. uses long-term asset funds, or LTAFs, while the EU's European long-term investment funds, known as ELTIFs, can be marketed across member states.

In the U.S., interval funds provide periodic, rather than daily, liquidity. Singapore, Japan and Hong Kong have also taken steps to broaden retail or listed access to private assets. The frameworks differ, but all aim to make less-liquid investments workable within existing distribution, suitability and liquidity requirements.

Yen Sum, global chair of the private capital practice at Latham & Watkins, and a member of its executive committee, said access to private-market funds is generally limited to investors who certify they are qualified or sophisticated.

She told CNBC on Thursday that the key issue for investor education centers on how private assets differ in liquidity, maturities and return-of-capital terms, and how higher rates can pressure leveraged companies — rather than simply whether regulation should curb access.

#2849. Rheinmetall reins in growth ambitions as Europe's rearmament boom hits hurdles

Rheinmetall is adopting a more "cautious" approach after the German arms maker lost a major contract to build F126 warships for the country's military, in what would have been Rheinmetall's biggest order since World War II.

The tank and ammunition maker now expects sales of between 33 and 37 billion euros (between $36.2 billion and $40.6 billion) by 2030, down from a previous forecast of about 45 billion euros, it told investors at its Capital Markets Day on Friday.

That guidance incorporates a "more prudent approach" for its large vehicle systems and weapons and ammunition businesses.

CEO Armin Papperger said a 2-billion-euro reduction in the 2030 sales target for its vehicle systems business was a strategic decision to build in a "buffer" against potential project delays and supply chain disruptions, rather than a reflection of further lost contracts or weakening demand.

It follows a full-year 2026 guidance cut earlier this month, which sent shares down 16% as the company cited delays to military contracts and ammunition deliveries.

"The opportunity has not changed; the way we plan for it has," Rheinmetall said in a company presentation.

"We are becoming more cautious whilst retaining our agility in a dynamic market," it said, highlighting a "new normal level of conservatism and recalibrated ambition" in the mid-term.

Shares rose 4% in mid-morning trading amid a European defense sector firmly in the green.

Rheinmetall and the wider defense industry have benefitted massively from European governments' efforts to rearm amid the war in Ukraine and uncertain security guarantees from the U.S. As governments ramp up military spending to meet NATO's hiked spending targets, defense companies see swelling order books but also struggle to meet soaring demand.

While shares of Rheinmetall have averaged triple-digit gains between 2022 and 2025, they are down 49% year-to-date. Addressing capital markets specifically, Papperger said he understood the disappointment, but that the loss of the major F126 was "a political thing."

"The government made this decision, and the minister made that decision," he said.

Other headwinds for Rheinmetall in 2026 were an explosion at an ammunition factory in Murcia, Spain, in January, and delays to Germany's Arminius project, a major military procurement program for Boxer armored vehicles, which is now expected to be commissioned next year.

Papperger said delays were due to price negotiations with the government. "I could have the contract immediately if I would reduce the prices, he said.

"We are fighting hard to get this contract, and we are fighting hard, not only to get the contract, but to make it also profitable."

RBC Capital analysts called the lowered mid-term targets a "widely expected reset" with "a general dose of conservatism."

"[The] emphasis on digitalisation offerings and the broadness of the portfolio should also help address sentiment around Rheinmetall being an "old-tech" name in a "new-tech" paradigm," the analysts added.

Some investors are now questioning what role legacy defense contractors like Rheinmetall, BAE Systems, and Leonardo will play in an evolving military context, increasingly focused on technology, autonomous systems, and drones.

Papperger pushed back on the idea that drones will render traditional military equipment obsolete, arguing that militaries must still replace their aging fleets and maintain the ability to transport troops on the battlefield.

"Don't believe what a lot of people [are] telling you in the narrative 'we only need drones.' Forget about that," Papperger said. "The market potential is still there."

Rheinmetall on Friday also warned that Europe faces mounting security threats beyond conventional warfare, pointing to an increase in hybrid attacks ranging from industrial sabotage and cyber espionage to airspace disruption and satellite jamming.

#2847. German software firm SAP is reportedly cutting at least 10,000 jobs in AI push

SAP is preparing a plan to eliminate at least 10,000 positions as the German firm reshapes its software business for the artificial intelligence era, Bloomberg reported on Thursday, citing people familiar with the matter.

"The business environment in which we operate is changing rapidly through AI, and we are continuously reviewing how our organization and workforce need to evolve to remain successful," SAP said in a statement to CNBC.

"As part of this ongoing transformation, roles and functions will naturally change over time. Beyond this, no decisions have been made regarding any further measures. We do not comment on speculation."

The company did not provide any further details on potential job cuts.

The report came as the company flagged a cybersecurity attack, but said it had found "no evidence of unauthorized access to customer business data."

"SAP has identified suspicious activity and promptly implemented the necessary measures to protect its customers and preserve the security of its services," a company spokesperson said.

"Our customers trust us to protect their data, and that remains our highest priority. In a highly dynamic threat environment, we will continue to strengthen our security to provide the secure and resilient environment our customers depend on."

Other software companies have also tied workforce reductions to AI initiatives, though for different reasons. WiseTech Global said in February it planned to cut about 2,000 jobs over two years as it integrated AI into its products and operations.

Atlassian said in March it would cut about 1,600 jobs partly to fund further AI investment, while Cloudflare said in May it would eliminate more than 1,100 roles as it moved toward an AI-first operating model.

In the U.S., employers cited AI in 121,401 job-cut announcements through October 2026, accounting for about 20% of the total, according to a Nov. 5 report from outplacement and executive coaching firm Challenger, Gray & Christmas.

SAP employs more than 110,000 people worldwide. Bloomberg reported that all departments had been asked to identify potential cuts, with product, engineering and sales in particular focus.

The company also underwent a reorganization in 2024, when it eliminated around 10,000 positions through a program that relied mainly on voluntary departures and retraining.

Its headcount later remained relatively flat as it hired for new roles, Bloomberg reported.

In 2023, SAP CEO Christian Klein told CNBC he did not necessarily expect a reduction in the workforce, saying he expected the company to grow and "accelerate" its total revenue.

The reported cuts come as Klein pushes SAP toward an AI-first strategy. This year, SAP completed four acquisitions — Reltio, Dremio, Prior Labs and TechWolf — and made strategic investments in AI-focused companies including Parloa, n8n and Conduct.

The acquisitions are intended to help SAP organize company data, develop AI models for business information and analyze workforce skills.
