#2846. Lights-out Luka and contagious chemistry: are these Lakers for real?

One of the components of Luka Dončić's shock trade from the Dallas Mavericks to the Los Angeles Lakers that caused great consternation was that he was just entering his prime as an NBA superstar. The worry was that the move could end that prime before it really even began – unceremoniously uprooting the Slovenian wunderkind from a team custom built around his strengths, fresh off a run to the NBA finals, to one who didn't really make sense at all. Sure, the bright lights of Hollywood would elevate Dončić's profile and usher him into a new echelon of stardom. But with Oklahoma City starting their run of dominance and rising stars around the league waiting in the wings, some believed his hopes of title contention (or even a long-elusive MVP award) had vanished.

Related: Victor Wembanyama has turned his NBA finals humiliation into a revenge tour for the ages

But the start to this season raises the question: what if they were wrong? Dončić is neck-and-neck with Victor Wembanyama in the MVP race, and the Lakers have the best record in the NBA at 15-3, with the Spurs and 76ers just behind at 15-4. San Antonio and their gargantuan French superstar were title and MVP favorites by a wide margin entering this season. But Dončić and the Lakers are keeping pace by pretty much every margin. Friday night's NBA Cup showdown will be a duel with ripple effects and stakes rarely seen in the regular season, let alone in November. The two top records. The two leading MVP candidates. A basketball fan couldn't ask for much more.

While there's a general consensus, at least for now, that the MVP race will come down to Dončić and Wembanyama, pundits have been more reluctant to acknowledge the new-look Lakers' emerging contenderdom. They have hovered near or occupied first place in the Western Conference throughout the season's first month, and they are top-11 in both offensive and defensive rating. Those are usually the hallmarks of a bona fide contender, but the skepticism persists.

Maybe that's because there was some dubiousness about the Lakers' brass pushing their chips in this summer on Walker Kessler, the 25-year-old with alluring talent but a limited résumé, whom the team have slotted as their center of the future. Or perhaps it's that, aside from Dončić and walking basketball Cinderella story Austin Reaves (who has been sidelined with a heel injury but is slated to return against San Antonio), the roster is lacking in household names. Many of their players, from shooting guard Quentin Grimes to forward Sandro Mamukelashvili, are NBA journeymen, having spent years bouncing around the league looking for the right home.

The Lakers turned over nine of their 15 roster spots this summer, an unusually drastic move for a team coming off a trip to the conference semi-finals and a second straight season with at least 50 wins. But they were committed to building a team around Dončić, and that required a bold approach to overhaul. JJ Redick, the team's head coach and someone who reportedly played a substantial role in handpicking the roster, acknowledged that turning over essentially the entire team in one offseason is not without its challenges, especially in a market like Los Angeles, where excellence is the goal and immediacy is the decree. "I made this comment to the team after we lost to the Warriors [on 30 October, one of their three defeats to date]: 'We've played six games together. Draymond [Green] and Steph [Curry] have played 13 years together,'" he recalled to me. "Patience is really important. I think one of the things I said in Rob [Pelinka's] and in my press conference right before Media Day was the belief that this team can become a great team. And that requires patience, that requires a lot of coaching and teaching and buy-in. So that whole process takes time. And you also have to do all of that while playing every other day, or five games in seven nights, and getting results stamped on your forehead every 24 hours. As a human, that's just hard to do."

The Lakers, by any objective measure, are becoming a great team. They may not be one yet (the bout with the Spurs will be their first real test after a soft early schedule) but they appear well on their way. There are several factors to point to – chief among them Dončić's historic scoring pace – but buy-in and chemistry should not be overlooked. It started, Redick and several team members tell me, on a team bonding trip to Slovenia this summer where Dončić began to level up as a leader. He self-funded the trip, expediting the bonding process with such a new group while also letting his teammates see his origin story. It was widely appreciated, and proved effective.

With a group of eager role-players immediately willing to buy into Dončić and Redick's vision, friction has been at a minimum, and roles slotted into with relative ease. "When we started training camp, I saw a lot of potential in this team," Dončić said after an overtime win against the Charlotte Hornets. "At the end of the day we have great chemistry, which is, in my opinion, very important. But I think this group is very special." His teammate Collin Sexton, one of the aforementioned journeymen, agrees. "We're gelling a lot faster than anyone could've expected. That's something that's helping us win games," he said. "That camaraderie, and that togetherness is something that's going to continue to allow us to expedite the friendships on the court, but also off the court."

Related: Giannis says Dončić could be best scorer of all time as Lakers star pours in 42 against Heat

I asked Redick why he thinks the group has been able to gel so quickly. He immediately recalled a team dinner he hosted in Las Vegas at Carbone Riviera, the night before a preseason contest in October. It was a two-plus-hour dinner, and when the checks came he looked around and noticed something. "I get up to leave, and it struck me: nobody had left. Nobody had been on their phone," he said. "Part of this, beyond just the time spent together [in Slovenia and in the lead-up to the season], is the people in there – the people in that locker room. It's just, they want to be part of a special group."

Fresh off two "successful" regular seasons that ended in playoff disappointment, Redick makes a point to acknowledge that it's still early: "I don't know what this is going to look like after 82 games. I don't know what it's going to look like after 50. But I do know that there's something with this group. And every special group doesn't mean you win a championship. But it's a special group of guys."

No contender can summit the mountaintop on the power of friendship alone, so it really does, ultimately, come back to Dončić's brilliance. His teammates understand this, and are leaning into their jobs as supporting actors in his quest for his first proverbial Best Actor and Best Picture. Kessler, chuckling and shaking his head after another night of his teammate's dominance, stated definitively after Dončić's 53-point outburst against the Portland Trail Blazers this month: "He's my MVP, I think it's pretty clear right now."

Time will tell if these Lakers really have what it takes to win it all. But in a wide-open Western Conference, with everything seemingly falling into place faster than most imagined, they're operating with the ethos of a popular refrain: "Why not us?"

#2845. Ohio State football officially eliminated from Big Ten title game contention

Ohio State has been eliminated from contention for the Big Ten championship game.

While the Buckeyes had lost three of four games entering the last week of the regular season, they remained mathematically alive to return to the conference title game.

Ohio State celebrates its win over Michigan in 1954.

Ohio State Buckeyes running back Ezekiel Elliott (15) takes a selfie with fans after the Buckeyes beat the Michigan Wolverines 42-13 at Michigan Stadium in Arbor, Michigan on November 28, 2015.

Ohio State celebrates its win over Michigan in 1954.

Ohio State Buckeyes running back Ezekiel Elliott (15) takes a selfie with fans after the Buckeyes beat the Michigan Wolverines 42-13 at Michigan Stadium in Arbor, Michigan on November 28, 2015.

But the one remote path to Indianapolis required an extreme set of circumstances that not only involved Ohio State defeating archrival Michigan, but also getting help across the league such as the Hawkeyes losing to the Cornhuskers.

The Buckeyes have not won the Big Ten since they captured four straight conference championships from 2017-20.

#2844. Planes, trains and automobiles: The best ways to beat travel fatigue—so you can come home feeling rested and fulfilled

Five-hour flights. Eleven-hour road trips. Fifteen-hour train rides. All potentially exhausting options for the annual tradition of convening around the dinner table with loved ones on Thanksgiving.

An estimated 81.5 million Americans are projected to travel, or have already traveled, at least 50 miles from their homes between Nov. 24 and Nov. 30, according to the American Automobile Association. And their mode of transportation is shifting: Fewer people are flying or driving this year compared to 2025, and more are traveling by buses, trains and cruises, AAA data shows.

High gas prices and soaring plane ticket costs may factor into the shift. As of Wednesday evening, fuel prices are roughly 17% higher than they were on the same date in 2025, according to AAA data. Meanwhile, "months of elevated jet fuel costs due to the conflict in Iran have contributed to Thanksgiving and Christmas airfares reaching 10-year highs," according to fare-tracking platform Hopper.

The alternate modes of transit may open travelers up to new experiences — and new forms of travel fatigue to keep you from returning home feeling rested and fulfilled. You're probably already familiar with the exhaustion you might get from a day of plane travel, for example. In airplanes, "there can be lower humidity, maybe lower oxygen levels at cabin pressure. Sometimes people report feeling more dehydrated or drained. You're adjusting to that change in altitude," says Alaina Tiani, a Cleveland Clinic behavioral sleep medicine psychologist.

But even waiting in places like train stations or cruise ports can feel draining due to sheer sensory overload, Tiani says. And then, depending on your mode of travel, you may still need to deal with cramped seating and limited ability to get up and walk around or drink some water and eat a snack.

Once you arrive at your destination, managing responsibilities like remembering where you put your hotel key and deciding what to eat and how to structure your day can also take a mental toll, says travel writer Shannon O'Donnell, who's written about managing travel fatigue. "All of that actually requires a lot more thinking and planning for your brain," she says, adding: "People just don't understand how those add up and why a relaxing vacation suddenly has them feeling wrecked."

The consequences can sneak up on you, O'Donnell notes. Travel fatigue can "present as maybe a lack of interest in something that you might usually find fascinating," she says. Instead of looking out the window on a plane ride or playing car games with your kids on a road trip, you might disengage and appear apathetic to the people around you.

You might stand a better chance of a fulfilling, restful holiday if you prepare for your travel fatigue. Here, Tiani and O'Donnell share their advice for how to do it:

#2842. What is ‘texture-maxxing’? The food trend putting crunch on the menu

Texture-maxxing is the latest food trend taking social media by storm as consumers, driven by aesthetics and taste, look for a sensory experience.

British supermarket Waitrose's latest food and drink report highlighted "texture-maxxing" as one of the trends of the year. It describes the phenomenon as a multi-sensory indulgence that is "fuelled by texture-maxxing crunch and intense flavour hits," pushing shoppers to fill their trolleys with crunchy peanut butter, dumplings, and trifles.

"This year, the sound of food went mainstream," Waitrose's Senior Development Chef Will Torrent said in the Wednesday report. "Driven by social media's obsession with audible, multi-sensory cooking, we're creating high-contrast meals that combine shatter-crisp crunch with pillowy chew."

"The emphasis in social media content has flipped from narrative voiceovers to ASMR-inspired audio, where the evocative sounds of food are just as important as the visuals," he added.

Food trends on social media have ranged from the obvious to the absurd. Fibermaxxing and proteinmaxxing have taken off in the past few years. Meanwhile, food and beverages like Dubai chocolate, protein coffee, and ube were some of the big social media hits.

Nestlé's USA 2026 trends found that 75% of Gen Z and 80% of millennials sought out texture in snack cravings with the hashtag "crunchtok," amassing 1.5 billion views.

Waitrose noted that shoppers looking to maximize on texture bought its viral No.1 Alphonso Mango Dessert, which involves "cracking through the crisp shell" to access a "smooth mango mousse and passion fruit curd on a crunchy white chocolate base."

Waitrose's TikTok and Instagram videos of eating the dessert were watched by more than two million people, with a whopping 6,173 hours and 36 minutes of play time on TikTok. Its videos featuring crunchy toast also went viral.

The U.K. grocer's sales of jarred chili crunches, which include crispy rayu oils such as itsu Crispy Chilli Oil, saw a 13-fold jump. Meanwhile, crunchy peanut butter outsold smooth.

Dumplings also had a moment, with sales of chicken gyoza dumplings up 116% this year and searches on waitrose.com for gochujang and peanut butter udon noodles jumped 60%.

"We've seen an explosion in demand as shoppers treat dumplings not just as a quick weeknight bite, but as a total sensory experience," Waitrose's Executive Chef Stephen Parkins-Knight said.

"Driven by the rise of texture-maxxing, people want that ultimate contrast: delicate skins, juicy fillings and crispy skirts, topped with very chilli crunch. With innovation ranging from fusion fillings to rich dipping broths, dumplings have taken a delicious centre stage in 2026," he added.

Texture-maxxing also extended to desserts, as shoppers bought twice as many chocolate trifles compared to 2025, and sales of strawberry trifles increased 160%.

Tiramisu was also popular, with searches for waitrose.com's tiramisu recipe page increasing 155% year-on-year.

#2841. Aaron Rodgers got no support from the Thanksgiving broadcasts

The day after former Steelers quarterback Aaron Rodgers went on TV and aired dirty laundry from the Pittsburgh locker room, none of the former quarterbacks (or other former players) who appear on TV supported him.

Across the board, the reaction from the NFL's analysts and broadcasters was negative.

Former Steelers quarterback and Fox analyst Terry Bradshaw went off. Fox's Michael Strahan agreed that Rodgers shouldn't have gone public with his complaints — calling him a "narcissist" and a "rich jerk" and a "rich bad teammate."

Fox's Howie Long said that the moment "defines who [Rodgers] is."

"He's a guy that I think picked the wrong sport," Long said. "He probably should have played tennis."

Fox's Rob Gronkowski said that, even if a contender loses its starting quarterback, there's "too much smoke" to justify bringing Rodgers in.

The CBS pregame crew offered similar views. Nate Burleson said Rodgers "didn't handle this right." Bill Cowher said he hates to see a Hall of Famer go out this way. Russell Wilson said, "It's about Aaron Rodgers's legacy. . . . If I'm his family members, if I'm somebody close to him, I'm his significant other, whatever it may be. 'Is this how you want to go out?' After all the success he's had, is this how you want to go out? . . . This is a disappointment."

During the Eagles-Cowboys game, Fox's Tom Brady criticized Rodgers for violating the "sanctity" of the locker room.

The irony, if I'm using the term correctly (I never know), is that Rodgers has previously railed about leaks from the locker room.

"I think it's chickenshit at its core, and I think it has no place in a winning organization," Rodgers said about the Jets.

"One thing I really love — and it's kind of the antithesis of where I was — is there's not really any leaks in the boat," Rodgers told reporters during his first year with the Steelers. "Every year, you have difficulties and adversity, both on the field and off the field, and to go through a season like this, and to be able to focus on football and not have a lot of other little bullshit out there has been really nice."

Rodgers didn't leak anything. He said it directly, bluntly, unapologetically. He took the team's focus off of football, at a time when the team was otherwise trying to fight through "difficulties and adversity."

Again, he isn't a whistleblower. He's a troublemaker. He didn't get his way, so he set the house on fire as he walked out the front door.

As one PFT reader put it, ultimatums and "blackmail" are close cousins. For Rodgers, it wasn't just "let me call the plays or cut me." It was, whether he said it this way or not, "let me call the plays or I'll tell the world all the stuff you don't want the world to know."

#2839. AMD dives on short seller report of alleged China exposure

Shares of chipmaker Advanced Micro Devices close down 7.72% to $489.19 on Thursday after investment firm Hunterbrook Media issued a report alleging that the company "has its own China problem."

Hunterbrook disclosed that it had taken a short position in AMD.

The report claims that "after the U.S. blacklisted Chinese server maker IEIT Systems, the company kept getting newer AMD firmware, which enables AMD chips to run in servers."

CNBC did not independently verify this information.

Hunterbrook also said that new export rules in Washington could "tighten restrictions on AMD's foreign business," alleging that AMD "bills over 20% of its revenue to customers in China and Hong Kong," and "depends on a Chinese partner to finish most of its chips."

The allegations come amid investigations of rival chipmaker Nvidia over its sales to companies in Asia with ties to China. AMD has been viewed as a beneficiary of the uncertainty surrounding Nvidia.

"The claims misrepresent AMD's business practices and relationships," an AMD spokesperson said in a statement. "AMD complies with applicable US export control laws and regulations and maintains rigorous processes to ensure compliance across our global operations. We remain focused on serving our customers and upholding the highest standards of integrity."

Lisa Su, CEO of AMD, told investors on the company's recent earnings call that China is an "important" market and that the semiconductor giant wants to be fully compliant with U.S. export controls.

AMD continued to slide after hours, trading down 0.45% to $487 shortly after 4 p.m.

#2838. Egg Bowl 2026: Winners, Losers from Ole Miss Rebels’ 41-17 Victory Over Mississippi State

Rivalry Week kicked off on Friday, with the 2026 Egg Bowl between the Mississippi State Bulldogs and Ole Miss Rebels in Oxford. Mississippi has dominated this in-state battle as of late, winning the last three and it is 67-46-6 overall entering today, with Friday afternoon's victory over the Bulldogs adding to that historic excellence over its biggest rival.

Let's dive into our winners and losers from the 2026 Egg Bowl, with Ole Miss putting up 35 points in the second half to come out on top with a 41-17 victory.

Nov 27, 2026; Oxford, Mississippi, USA; Mississippi State Bulldogs quarterback Parker Puckett (15) looks to throw during the first quarter against the Mississippi Rebels in the Egg Bowl at Vaught-Hemingway Stadium.at Vaught-Hemingway Stadium. Mandatory Credit: Petre Thomas-Imagn Images

Kamario Taylor might be the $7.5 million man for Mississippi State, but credit today must be given to Parker Puckett. He was highly efficient in the first half in a hostile environment, completing 11-of-13 passes for 104 yards (8 yards per attempt), and that effectiveness through the air proved instrumental in a pair of second-quarter scoring drives that gave Mississippi State the lead at halftime. Unfortunately, the fill-in starter made one costly mistake, forcing a pass that was easily read by Blake Purchase near the line of scrimmage and returned 58 yards to set up a 1-yard touchdown a play later. Puckett's first-half performance helped make this a close game and his fourth-quarter implosion played a role in it becoming a one-sided contest.

Nov 27, 2026; Oxford, Mississippi, USA; Mississippi Rebels quarterback Trinidad Chambliss (6) throws against Mississippi State Bulldogs defensive lineman Will Whitson (43) and linebacker Fatt Forrest (0) during the first quarter at Vaught-Hemingway Stadium. Mandatory Credit: Petre Thomas-Imagn Images

Entering the Egg Bowl, the Bulldogs defense ranked 106th nationally in points allowed in the first half (20.0) per game over its last three contests. The Ole Miss offense managed just six first-half points, reaching the red zone twice on its first four drives only to have penalties wipe out a touchdown and ruin great field position. Getting outscored (10-6) and outgained (148 total yards to 131) in the first half by a Kamario Taylor-less Bulldogs offense is just an awful look for the Rebels.

Oct 31, 2026; Oxford, Mississippi, USA; Mississippi Rebels linebacker Luke Ferrelli (12) looks on during the first quarter against the Auburn Tigers at Vaught-Hemingway Stadium. Mandatory Credit: Petre Thomas-Imagn Images

Playing in the Egg Bowl for the first time, sophomore linebacker Luke Ferrelli stepped up for this Rebels defense. The 6-foot-3 defender sacked Puckett and finished the first half with 1.5 tackles for loss, making several plays near the line of scrimmage. Considering the kind of day Mississippi's secondary had in coverage, Ferrelli was one of the few standouts on this side of the ball.

Oct 31, 2026; Oxford, Mississippi, USA; Mississippi Rebels wide receiver Horatio Fields (11) and wide receiver Samari Reed (81) react after a touchdown during the fourth quarter against the Auburn Tigers at Vaught-Hemingway Stadium. Mandatory Credit: Petre Thomas-Imagn Images

It's even more impressive that Trinidad Chambliss threw for 200 yards in the Egg Bowl considering how often his pass-catchers let him down. Mississippi's receivers and tight ends had seven drops on Friday against the Bulldogs, with several of those coming on what should've been third-down conversions. Deuce Alexander also didn't do much with double-digit targets, finishing under 50 receiving yards on seven receptions.

Ole Miss quarterback Trinidad Chambliss (6) throws during the Egg Bowl against Mississippi State at Vaught Hemingway Stadium in Oxford, Miss., on Friday, Nov. 27, 2026.

As we mentioned above, the Rebels' pass-catchers did Trinidad Chambliss no favors in this one and penalties only made things worse. He still made sure Mississippi came out on top of the Egg Bowl one final time as its starting quarterback. Chambliss accounted for three total touchdowns in the second half, also leading another touchdown drive that covered 78 yards on 10 plays in just three minutes.

Nov 27, 2026; Oxford, Mississippi, USA; Mississippi State Bulldogs head coach Jeff Lebby during the fourth quarter against the Mississippi Rebels in the Egg Bowl at Vaught-Hemingway Stadium. Mandatory Credit: Petre Thomas-Imagn Images

One reasonable litmus test of a head coach in college football is how their team performs without a star quarterback. The results for Jeff Lebby since he lost Kamario Taylor for the season have been brutal. Mississippi State was outscored 35-7 in the second half of the Egg Bowl, turning a competitive game for two quarters into another in a long line of ugly losses for Lebby's program. The Bulldogs were at one point 4-0 with a healthy Taylor, and they are going to end the 2026 season with a 5-7 record. That's one win, 34-16 over Tennessee Tech, and seven losses without Taylor as the full-time starter.

Oct 31, 2026; Oxford, Mississippi, USA; Mississippi Rebels defensive end Blake Purchase (3), linebacker Suntarine Perkins (4), defensive tackle Jayden Curtis (90) and linebacker Tah'j Butler (49) react after a sack during the fourth quarter against the Auburn Tigers at Vaught-Hemingway Stadium. Mandatory Credit: Petre Thomas-Imagn Images

It was utter domination from Mississippi's front seven in this game. While Suntarine Perkins (3 tackles for loss and 1.5 sacks) led the way, seven different Rebels defenders recorded a sack on Friday and nine had a tackle for loss with three of those players having multiple tackles for loss. Bulldogs running back Fluff Bothwell was able to put up 129 rushing yards (7.2 yards per carry), but this group was largely dominant and it took over the game in the second half.

#2834. Two Italians killed in Kuwait drone attack; Trump says Iran war ends ‘whenever I want it’

Two Italians have been killed after an alleged Iranian attack on Kuwait, Kuwaiti forces said Thursday.

In a post on X, Kuwait's army said the deaths followed Iranian attacks on the country, with the two Italians killed by falling missile debris. Two others were also injured.

In response, a spokesperson for Iran's military told state media that no offensive or military operations had been carried out in the area and that "these accusations should be viewed as a cover for continued malevolence and an attempt to exacerbate insecurity in the region" by U.S. and Israeli forces.

Iranian Foreign Minister Abbas Araghchi said on Telegram that he had spoken with his Italian counterpart, Antonio Tajani, and expressed regret over their deaths, while stressing that Tehran had no role or involvement in the incident.

"The incident is suspicious and looks like a precooked recipe for war," Araghchi wrote on X.

He also said Iran was ready to cooperate with the governments of Kuwait and Italy in investigating the incident.

Separately, Israel said Thursday that it was prepared to carry out strikes against Iran "at any moment," even without U.S. forces joining the effort, according to Israeli military spokesman Effie Defrin on Israeli broadcaster Kan.

The comments came after U.S. envoy Jared Kushner told a podcast Wednesday that Israel "needs to get out of the constant wartime mindset."

Israel has been pushing the Trump administration to back another offensive against Tehran, with Reuters reporting on Nov. 18 that Israeli spy chief Roman Gofman pushed for joint strikes when he met U.S. officials earlier in the month.

Late Thursday, President Donald Trump said on PBS that the U.S. had "victory at hand" in the Iran conflict and reiterated that victory would come "whenever I want it to be, to be honest."

Speaking during a Thanksgiving call with U.S. military personnel, Trump said Washington was still trying to give Iran "every chance to make a deal and save a lot of lives."

But he added that he was not sure whether Tehran would agree.

"Some people question why. Are they crazy? Maybe they are," Trump said.

#2833. Anthropic was supposed to save the AI trade. What went wrong?

Anthropic's historic IPO — the largest in U.S. history — delivered a big win for the company and its investors. But it failed at another task: reviving animal spirits in the AI trade.

The Nasdaq Composite closed down 2.25% amid a broader market pullback with the S&P 500 declining 2% to 7,175.

Investors had been looking forward to the Anthropic IPO as a "clearing event" — reviving the tech sector after months of choppy trading, the Nvidia espionage scandal, and growing concerns that AI chip sales to Asia are likely to slump as Washington cracks down on potential violations of export controls.

Some of those worries resurfaced Thursday as a short seller, Hunterbrook Media, alleged in a report that firmware sold by chipmaker AMD had turned up in U.S.-blacklisted companies. AMD stock closed down more than 7%. The chipmaker told CNBC it complies with U.S. law.

Adding to the worries, the House Foreign Affairs Committee released documents showing that a subagency of the Commerce Department approved the sale of more than 879,000 AI chips to Chinese companies. That also raised the prospect of an Asia sales slowdown under stricter export controls.

Many investors and analysts continue to express confidence in the AI trade, noting that Anthropic's successful market debut — the stock closed up 17% from its IPO price and raised more than $81 billion — bodes well for capital spending across tech.

But investors acknowledged that this is a high-tension moment and said it's time to be more strategic about the trade.

Dan Niles, founder of Niles Investment Management, described himself as a "circumspect bull," making the comparison to market conditions in 2022 when the big indices tanked in the back half of the year.

"You could have still made money that year. You just needed to have the right combination of longs and shorts," he said.

Part of the challenge, Niles said, has been a price war between the two cutting-edge frontier labs, Anthropic and OpenAI. That has posed a threat to the overall revenue and profit potential of AI and the capex going into it. Fierce competition on price is producing collective losses between the two top companies, he notes.

"The combined revenue growth for OpenAI plus Anthropic went from growing 75% sequentially in Q2 to 48% in Q3," he told CNBC. "The combined losses went from $7.9 billion to about $8.4 billion from Q2 to Q3."

While uncertainty is high now, some resolution may be on the horizon if a greater degree of commercial and regulatory clarity emerges over the next few months.

Trading experts also told CNBC that the tech sector's troubles Thursday were a more serious matter than Anthropic simply siphoning capital from other parts of the market.

"There's a reset of AI in general," said JJ Kinahan, senior vice president at Cboe Global Markets.

Investors for years have profited in tech by buying the dip, he said, but "there's been an attitude change that [you] can't buy things blindly and they'll all go up." Nvidia's ongoing declines have shaken confidence in the buy-the-dip strategy, he said.

Anthropic's next few trading days will be a key gauge of the market's strength. Friday will be a "wild card," Kinahan said. It could be a "final flush out" for the market, or a bounce that leaves the market in "no man's land" while volatility remains elevated.

A better gauge may be Monday, when options on Anthropic start trading, providing insight into whether retail investors will back the company in force — providing more of a vote of confidence in the AI trade.

"If people think we're going up [on Anthropic] they'll buy stock, but retail particularly will just buy calls," he said. "There's a bigger test coming Monday."

For now, investors continue to sell AI-related stocks.

Neoclouds, the generally highly indebted AI-dedicated computing providers at the outer edges of the AI trade, were down substantially Thursday, with CoreWeave closing down more than 6%.

Hyperscalers such as Alphabet and Amazon.com, viewed as relatively defensive AI bets, also finished lower.

One bright spot: Cybersecurity stocks continued to rally, with Palo Alto Networks and CrowdStrike both closing up. AI is finding security holes across software infrastructure, creating a scramble to patch the vulnerabilities before hackers can exploit them.

— CNBC's Daren Fonda contributed reporting.

#2832. Chiefs Special Teams Collapse Cost Them In Buffalo

The Kansas City Chiefs lost a close game in Buffalo on Thursday, Nov. 26, and the blame lands squarely on special teams. The team gave up a 57-yard field goal as time expired, a play that sealed a 13-10 defeat. While the defense held Josh Allen to just 81 passing yards, the kicking unit failed to do its job. Per KSHB 41 Kansas City, the Chiefs' special teams unit committed a litany of miscues that doomed the team from the start.

Bad starts killed the offense before it could get going. Wide receiver Josh Downs let a 61-yard punt roll inside the 5-yard line instead of catching it. This mistake pinned the team deep and set a terrible tone. Seven of the Chiefs' 10 drives began at their own 20-yard line or worse. Matt Araiza, the punter, averaged a season-worst 41.6 yards per punt and dropped a snap that led to a 13-yard boot. Dave Toub has led the unit since 2013, but the play on this night was sloppy. Coach Andy Reid admitted the group needs to fix these issues. Reid said, "We've got to do better," when asked about his confidence in the staff.

The final play was a direct result of earlier failures. With seconds left, the Chiefs punted from their own end zone. A Bills player rushed the punter and got a hand on the ball. The punt was tipped, leaving it low and short. This set up the game-winning kick for Tyler Bass. Bass hit the 57-yard bomb through the rain and wind. He noted the brutal conditions, stating, "I've played in a lot of bad conditions here; [That] definitely was up there as far as conditions-wise." The Bills special teams unit capitalized on every mistake, while the Chiefs left three points on the board with a missed field goal by Harrison Butker earlier in the fourth quarter.

Despite the Chiefs gaining more total yards than the Bills, they lost the field position battle. Reid emphasized that the team cannot go backwards in tight games against good opponents. The team now heads to Los Angeles for their next matchup on Thursday, Dec. 3, at 8:15 p.m. ET.

#2831. It’s not just you: Durable goods are getting less reliable, from sputtering cars to glitching smart TVs

"They just don't make things like they used to." The phrase, formerly exclusive to grandparents, is now used by consumers of all ages.

Across the United States, durable goods like cars, appliances and electronics are becoming less reliable. In the automobile industry, owners of 3-year-old models reported an average of 204 problems per 100 vehicles as of Feb. 12, the highest level since 2022, according to a report from consumer intelligence company J.D. Power. Reliability is a growing concern for dishwashers and front-load washers too, respectively experiencing an average of 92 and 91 problems per 100 units as of Oct. 29, according to a similar J.D. Power report.

Consumers have noticed: Shoppers dealing with high inflation rates and added tariffs on imported goods are unsatisfied with all kinds of products, from cars and tech devices to furniture and kitchen gear. Just over three-quarters of Americans say they believe companies often sell lower-quality products and services at higher prices, according to an Axios and Harris Poll survey published in May 2025.

To be sure, reliability and durability aren't the only ways to define a product's quality. Aesthetics and performance matter to consumers, too. Your television today may not last as long as the one you grew up with, but it's probably larger and thinner with better picture definition — and it likely costs much less, when adjusted for inflation, says Justin Sirotin, an industrial design professor at the Rhode Island School of Design. Indeed, the average price of a television was roughly $622 in 1996 adjusted for inflation, whereas a 55-inch LCD model now costs $232 on average.

But because they're penny-pinched, consumers are becoming more focused on finding high-value products — ones that perform consistently and reliably — than on price, according to a 2025 Deloitte report.

Unfortunately for shoppers, some businesses are also economically squeezed, and may manufacture products with cheaper, less durable parts to keep their profits growing, Sirotin says. Plus, we've all gotten used to sleek products with impressive features at affordable prices, and when businesses have to deliver on those aspects to grow demand, longevity can drop down the list of priorities. Manufacturers may prioritize quality on the external parts of a product — like the picture quality on a television — and get their cost savings with cheaper internal components that you won't initially notice, says Keyana Sapp, an investigative journalist and creator of the Worse on Purpose newsletter.

In some cases, a product's durability may even be part of a "planned obsolescence," wherein businesses intentionally give goods a certain lifespan so customers need to buy replacements within a predictable timeframe. Planned obsolescence isn't necessarily rampant, and it's hard to prove that any individual company purposefully designs products to fail, Sapp says.

But historically speaking, it's not entirely a conspiracy theory. In 1924, major light bulb manufacturers formed an international coalition and agreed to lower and standardize the lifespan of incandescent light bulbs to 1,000 hours. The cartel broke up during World War II.

You can, of course, try extending the longevity of products you've already bought by paying to repair them, rather than getting a brand-new replacement. But as devices like vacuum cleaners and headphones evolve technologically, they require increasingly specialized tools and labor, says Shahram Mokhtari, lead teardown technician at online repair community and e-commerce site iFixit. Specialized screws, glued components and limited access to repair information can make some products difficult or impossible to fix, the Federal Trade Commission reported in May 2021.

Europe has right-to-repair regulations that require manufacturers of certain products to provide access to replacement parts, tools and information. Only nine U.S. states have similar legislation for consumer electronics.

If you want to know how long a product might last before you buy it, look up its reviews or do some research to understand if it's complicated to fix, Mokhtari recommends. Or, if you have the budget, you can prioritize higher-priced products that might bring both initial quality and long-term longevity. This may require intentional long-term planning for some consumers — even as online shopping has made impulse buying easier, Sirotin notes.

"It takes a lot of intentionality by the consumer to say, 'OK, well, is this chair really a chair that I want to have for 25 years? Or is this the chair I'm going to buy and sit in for six months, and then I'm just going to get another chair?'" says Sirotin.

#2829. What If Wolves: Ice, Ice, Jimmy!

"What If" Wolves Volume 8: A winning coach? What a novel concept.

Jet Helmbrecht: Dumpster fire, car wreck, cluster f&@$, hot mess, or an unholy cacophony of chaos. These are just a few phrases that, up until recently, perfectly describe the Minnesota Timberwolves Head Coaches through the years. Let's dive into that flaming dumpster, and I'll guide you through a bit of Wolves coaching history.

Since the franchise's inception in 1989, the Minnesota Timberwolves have had 16 different head coaching tenures. This includes two separate stints by Flip Saunders and Kevin McHale (whom I still hate). Let's do some quick math. 38 seasons divided by 16 different coaching stints comes out to a new coach every 2.375 seasons. Holy crap! I knew it was bad, but now that I see the actual number. Yikes!

PHOENIX – DECEMBER 15: Head coach Kurt Rambis of the Minnesota Timberwolves reacts during the NBA game against the Phoenix Suns at US Airways Center on December 15, 2010 in Phoenix, Arizona. The Suns defeated the Timberwolves 128-122. NOTE TO USER: User expressly acknowledges and agrees that, by downloading and or using this photograph, User is consenting to the terms and conditions of the Getty Images License Agreement. (Photo by Christian Petersen/Getty Images) | Getty Images

Looking over the past coaches, two things stood out right away. First, barely any of these guys had been a head coach before the Wolves hired them. Second, every coach has an overall losing record, apart from two: Flip Saunders and Chris Finch.

The first point can probably be traced back to ownership being cheap. Hiring inexperienced coaches is one way to save a few bucks. Out of all the Wolves coaches, only six had been a head coach before the Timberwolves. That list is Bill Musselman (Cleveland), Jimmy Rodgers (Boston Celtics), Randy Wittman (Cleveland), Rick Adelman (four different stops), Sam Mitchell (Toronto), and Tom Thibodeau (Chicago). The rest of the coaches were lifelong assistants, college coaches, "one of us" hires, nepo babies, or the dumbest coach to ever set foot on a basketball court, Kurt Rambis, who was so bad that his GM sold draft picks until he could buy him out. David Kahn rewrote the rules of basketball!!

Due to most of the coaches being inexperienced, the Wolves' poor draft record (some of which I had previously covered here, here, and here), and free agents basically ignoring the team, the losing records start to come into context. I can tell you that not all of the coaches were that bad. Some have won Coach of the Year at some point during their careers (Dwane Casey, Sam Mitchell, Thibs x2), and Adelman is in the Hall of Fame. But even these coaches had losing records with the Timberwolves.

I mentioned earlier that only Flip (427 wins – 392 losses) and Finch (269-198) have winning records with the Timberwolves. They are also the only coaches to lead the team to at least one series win in the playoffs. I loved watching Flip's teams back in the day, and now I never miss a game that Finchy is coaching. But the "What if" Wolves need to win some championships. Time to use the power of hindsight to make some adjustments to Wolves history and see if we can win a chip or two.

Remember, this is all for fun and to exercise the demons that are lurking around in my basketball-obsessed head. Also, sorry, Lynx fans. Also also, again, bless you, Chris Finch, for this moment.

In the spring of 2016, the Wolves had just wrapped up their season, missing the playoffs for the twelfth season in a row. 12! Sam Mitchell was unable to find the magic that he had in Toronto that earned him the COTY back in 2007. The roster had exciting young players in Karl-Anthony Towns, Andrew Wiggins, and Zach LaVine. Things were starting to look up, but a coaching change was desperately needed.

Timberwolves owner Glen Taylor had hired consulting firm Korn Ferry to conduct a search for a new coach and someone to run the front office. At the time, there were some big names being bandied about. Scott Brooks had found success with the Oklahoma City Thunder, winning Coach of the Year in 2010, and leading the Thunder to the 2010-11 Western Conference Finals. Mark Jackson got a nibble after turning around the Golden State Warriors (despite some pretty disgusting opinions on gay people). Jeff Van Gundy was willing to leave the TV booth and start coaching again. But ultimately, Tom Thibodeau won the job of Timberwolves Head Coach.

As a cherry on top, Thibs was also given the role of President of Basketball Operations. This dual-hatted role was all the rage at the time. Coaches wanted control, and being the POBO or GM gave them control of the roster that they all coveted.

Around the NBA, Greg Popovich was in charge in San Antonio, Doc Rivers was running the Los Angeles Clippers, and Stan Van Gundy (Jeff's brother) was leading a rebuild of the Detroit Pistons. Thibs wanted that power, and he was in a good position to make that demand after guiding the Chicago Bulls back to the playoffs. Some people can thrive in both of those roles; Thibs was not one of them.

Tom Thobodeau as a Wolves Assistant Coach in 1989. That mullet is glorious!

When Thibodeau was hired, he set out to remake the Wolves in the image of his old teams, the Boston Celtics where he was the assistant coach that reshaped their defense around Kevin Garnett's unworldly ability, and the Chicago Bulls that relied on Jimmy Butler and Joakim Noah to beat up on opposing teams. The Wolves were not structured to be a defense-first team. The best players were not labeled as defensive stalwarts by any means.

To make the Wolves a defensive juggernaut, Thibs started making moves by drafting Kris Dunn, a defensive-first guard from Providence, with his first pick as the Wolves boss. After Dunn's rookie season, he would later trade Dunn, Zach LaVine, and the number seven overall pick that would become Lauri Markkanen to the Chicago Bulls for a Jimmy Butler reunion. Coach Thibodeau would then become my mortal enemy by trading Ricky Rubio to the Utah Jazz for a late first-round pick. The roster makeover would be finalized after the Wolves signed Jeff Teague and Taj Gibson.

I was crushed that Rubio was gone, but the Wolves started winning. They even made the playoffs, breaking the 12-season streak. The winning would be short-lived, however. A late collapse led to Minnesota falling from the third seed to fighting for a playoff spot on the last night of the season. Butler would come back from an injury to try and take on the Houston Rockets, but nobody could stop James Harden, and the series was over in five quick games. Butler's true colors would eventually show, and he would demand out. I will leave it up to Jeff Teague to explain what happened.

After all that drama, Thibs was fired just over two seasons into his 5-year, $40 million deal. He came in, yelled at everyone, drafted the wrong guard (Jamal Murray was taken two picks after Dunn), traded away a promising young talent along with a high draft pick for General Soreness, transformed the Wolves into the Minnesota Bulls, and gave away Rubio. I can still hear him in my nightmares yelling at KAT and Taj to "ice" the pick-and-roll coverage. Tom Thibodeau was a good coach. He was just in over his head as POBO and was too determined to win with "his guys" from his heyday with the Bulls.

I already mentioned a bunch of high-profile names that were up for the coaching gig before Thibs was hired. But one name was left off that list. The "What If" Wolves are not interested in hiring a coach who once came close to competing for a championship. We want proven winners.

In 2016, instead of hiring Korn Ferry, Glen Taylor should have just walked down the hallway for his next head coach. So, as the "What If" Wolves Overlord, I make the short stroll and hire Cheryl Reeve of the Minnesota Lynx as the next head coach and POBO of the Minnesota Timberwolves. Luckily, her opinions on queer people are far more commendable than that loser, Mark Jackson.

Again, sorry, Lynx fans. But have no fear. I have asked Canis Hoopus' resident Lynx expert, Mitchell Hansen, to put your fears to rest and provide some insight into how Coach Reeve would lead the Timberwolves to the championship and how the Lynx would be able to keep dominating the W.

Mitchell Hansen: Reeve is one of the best coaches the WNBA — and the game of basketball as a whole — has seen. She is ingrained in and invested in the Twin Cities community. She is Minnesota basketball. She is obsessed with the game and is incredibly knowledgeable and strategic with every decision that is made, and she has a unique ability to bring the best out of her players and coaching staff. Those characteristics alone lead me to believe that she could make the transition to the NBA and succeed.

Let's go back to the time Thibs was hired and led the Timberwolves. At that time, Reeve had already established herself as one of the best coaches in basketball, having led the Lynx to three WNBA titles (which would later turn into a WNBA-record-tying fourth championship) and was in the midst of one of the greatest dynasties the league has ever seen.

Reeve is honestly similar to Thibs in multiple ways. They both have an old-school approach and have had to change their philosophy a bit to address the "new wave" of playing style and athletes. However, their core characteristics are similar. They are both defensive-minded coaches who demand accountability, challenge their players, and have high expectations for excellence within their organizations and teams. Where Reeve could have made the biggest difference for the Wolves that we've seen with the Lynx is her ability to bring players together, establish a collective identity and build a culture that has a history of resulting in translating to on-court success.

Reeve has shown throughout her coaching career an ability to identify the strengths of her players and put them in positions to succeed while also challenging them to expand their games. Her experience working with some of the best post players in women's basketball could have been particularly beneficial for Karl-Anthony Towns, Andrew Wiggins, and Zach LaVine in their early days with the Wolves. At the very least, she would put them in situations to give them chances to succeed — which, you know, is something good coaches typically do.

Perhaps the biggest ripple effect of hiring Reeve would have been the Jimmy Butler trade. Without the connection between Thibs and Butler dating back to their time together with the Chicago Bulls, it's fair to wonder whether the Timberwolves would have ever pursued that trade in the first place. That's not to say Reeve would have been opposed to acquiring an established star to complement Minnesota's young talent — she helped do so in 2015 when the Lynx traded for Hall of Fame center Sylvia Fowles — but the Timberwolves might have been more inclined to commit to their young core rather than break it up.

The hiring of Reeve wouldn't have guaranteed the Timberwolves a championship like what was basically an every-other-year occurrence with the Lynx during their dynasty, but it could have provided the organization with something it had been desperately searching for throughout much of its existence: stability.

Now, with all that said, what would have happened to Reeve's role, both with the Timberwolves and the Lynx?

Perhaps the biggest impact of her moving over to the Timberwolves side would be on the team she has led successfully since arriving in Minnesota in 2010. In 2016, at the time that the Timberwolves were looking to hire for the job, Reeve and the Lynx were in the heart of their championship window. Maya Moore, Lindsay Whalen, Seimone Augustus, Rebbekah Brunson, and the aforementioned Fowles were dominating the WNBA and were looking for their fourth title to add to an impressive dynasty run.

Would Reeve have been willing to leave that? Or would she have attempted to coach both franchises at the same time? If you know Reeve, you know that the second option would have likely been her decision — or at least she would have tried her damnedest to make it work.

From a scheduling standpoint, at the time, with the WNBA schedule at just 34 games, it would have been doable. She would have had to rely on her assistant coaches at various points, notably during training camps and preseason action, but it could have been done. But from a realistic standpoint, that's not sustainable. If Reeve did that, it would have likely run up until the conclusion of the dynasty era, and she would have made the choice to go to one squad or the other and focus on just one.

But there's another avenue she could have gone — what if she decided to leave the Lynx in that championship window and officially moved over to the NBA to lead the Timberwolves? Who would have led the Lynx, and what would the Lynx have looked like upon her exit?

MINNEAPOLIS, MN – May 16: Head Coach Cheryl Reeve and Assistant Coaches Jim Petersen and Shelley Patterson of the Minnesota Lynx pose for portraits during 2013 Media Day on May 16, 2013 at the Minnesota Timberwolves and Lynx LifeTime Fitness Training Center at Target Center in Minneapolis, Minnesota. NOTE TO USER: User expressly acknowledges and agrees that, by downloading and or using this Photograph, user is consenting to the terms and conditions of the Getty Images License Agreement. Mandatory Copyright Notice: Copyright 2013 NBAE (Photo by David Sherman/NBAE via Getty Images) | NBAE via Getty Images

The first, most likely option would have been that Glen Taylor and the organization would have tried to get Jim Petersen out of the Timberwolves broadcast booth to take over as head coach. There is obvious familiarity there, with Petersen serving as an assistant under Reeve during numerous title-contending seasons. The other candidate could have been long-time Lynx assistant coach Shelley Patterson, potentially offering a more long-term solution than Petersen would have provided.

Regardless of who would have taken over, the leadership and chemistry already built inside that Lynx locker room likely would have kept the championship hopes alive for at least the next few seasons — I mean, the team was filled with five Hall of Fame players in the starting lineup. Not to diminish what Reeve built in any way or what the team did to work as a collective unit so flawlessly, but the talent on those teams was unreal.

The biggest impact of Reeve's departure to the Timberwolves would likely have come years later, when the Lynx were going through their post-dynasty transition period. Reeve did a miraculous job maneuvering that era while keeping the winning culture and chemistry intact, something that isn't easy to do when you're on the downswing from numerous title runs.

If Reeve moved over to the NBA, it's likely that we wouldn't have seen much of an impact on the Lynx dynasty run that essentially concluded in 2018 with four titles. But it could have drastically changed the way the Lynx look today, because Reeve has done an incredible job continuing to build and readjust the roster to be right there in the conversation seemingly every season.

Overall, that's what makes this hypothetical scenario so fascinating from the Timberwolves and Lynx organizations' perspective. By having Reeve take over the Timberwolves, the team would have acquired a capable coach to transform their organization. But simultaneously, it would be taking away the person responsible for transforming the Lynx on the other side of the organization.

The Timberwolves' gain might have been the Lynx's greatest loss.

Jet Helmbrecht: All great points, Mitchell. I would love to have seen what Coach Reeve could have done with the young core of KAT, Wiggs, and LaVine. Plus, I bet we would have drafted Markkanen to add to a soon-to-be dynamic offense. Titles? Maybe. Maybe not. Either way, I would no longer be traumatized by Thibs screaming "ice" for 48 minutes per game. Thanks, Mitchell!

#2828. 'The Traitors' ended with a $246,300 prize—here's who won and what they told us about spending it

When Shane Beatty, a contestant on NBC's "The Traitors: New Blood," deceived his fellow finalists, he walked away with the entire $246,300 prize. But the emotional toll of winning caught him by surprise.

"The second I had to reveal that I was a Traitor, everything hit me at once," Beatty, a 32-year-old ironworker from Staten Island, New York, tells CNBC Make It. "I had built genuine relationships with these people, and knowing that some of them were blindsided by my decision was difficult."

Now that he's won, Beatty has a plan for his money, starting with setting aside a sizable share for Uncle Sam. As for the rest, he's more interested in financial security than the trappings of newfound wealth.

After winning, Beatty doesn't expect his day-to-day life to change much.

"I'm still going to get up, go to work, and continue doing what I've been doing as a Local 580 ironworker," he says. "I love my career, and winning this money doesn't change that."

Beatty plans to pay for a wedding he and his fiancée previously struggled to afford, help his mother pay down debt and clear his own debts before saving the rest.

"What it does give me is an opportunity to take some financial pressure off, be a little more comfortable and put myself in a better position for the future," he says.

But before spending his winnings, Beatty is accounting for what could be his single biggest expense — taxes.

"First thing I'm gonna do with the money is put at least $100,000 in a separate bank account just so I can have it ready for Uncle Sam," he told Entertainment Weekly last week.

Setting aside $100,000 is a reasonable move given Beatty's potential tax liability, according to tax experts.

"Taxes are often an afterthought when winning big on a gameshow," says Riley Adams, a certified public accountant and founder of Sequoia Ridge CPAs. "So, it's definitely smart on his part to get ahead with his tax planning."

Game show winnings are generally taxed as ordinary income, meaning the prize money is added to a winner's other taxable income for the year. That can push a winner into higher tax brackets.

In addition to federal income taxes, Beatty, who lives in New Jersey, will have to account for state income taxes.

While Beatty did not disclose his income to CNBC Make It, ironworkers in the New York City metropolitan area typically earn around $100,000 a year, according to 2025 Bureau of Labor Statistics data.

Assuming Beatty lives in New Jersey, earns $100,000 from his job in New York in 2026, receives his winnings this year, files as a single taxpayer with no dependents and takes the standard deduction, here's how much of his prize money he could keep, according to Adams' calculations:

Under this scenario, Beatty's winnings would push him from the 22% federal income tax bracket into the 35% bracket. Factoring in state income taxes, an estimated 35% of his prize would go to taxes, according to Adams' calculations. Setting aside $100,000 would give Beatty a comfortable cushion under this scenario. His actual tax bill, however, will depend on his income, deductions and other financial circumstances.

Jim Guarino, a certified public accountant and managing director at Baker Newman Noyes in Woburn, Massachusetts, arrived at a similar estimate.

"Folks tend to get excited by the 'gross amount' of their total winnings," but Beatty is "extremely tax-aware" in recognizing how much less he'll actually get to keep after taxes, he says.

For Beatty, the money won't radically change his life, but it will give him greater financial security.

"Having a savings cushion and knowing I don't have to worry about living paycheck to paycheck or falling behind on bills means so much to me," he says. "I want to be responsible with this opportunity and make sure it gives me greater financial peace of mind."

#2827. UAlbany football ready to 'let it rip' as underdog at Lehigh in FCS playoffs

ALBANY - The University at Albany football team is used to being overlooked.

The Great Danes far exceeded preseason expectations by tying for second place in the Coastal Athletic Association. They defied predictions by squeezing into the NCAA Football Championship Subdivision playoff field.

First-year UAlbany head coach Tom Perkovich is embracing an underdog mentality heading into a first-round game against 12th-seeded Lehigh at Goodman Stadium in Bethlehem, Pa., on Saturday at noon.

"There's no one picking us to win, right?" Perkovich said. "There's people doubting us. I like being in that situation. I've been there before and overcome it. I think our team will be ready, we'll play hard and give our very best out there."

Patriot League champion Lehigh (10-2) is a 6 ½-point favorite over UAlbany (8-4), which received an at-large bid as one of the last four teams to make the 24-team field.

UAlbany senior defensive lineman Shawan Lewis watched the selection show last Sunday with his roommates. The Great Danes weren't expected to make it as a bubble team in bracket projections.

"It was just shocking, but we worked for it and as soon as I saw it, I was screaming, yelling, excited," Lewis said. "I get another chance to play more football, so it was an amazing feeling."

The Great Danes, who went 2-10 last season, were picked 11th in the CAA preseason coaches' poll.

UAlbany is making its first FCS playoff appearance since reaching the semifinals in 2023. Great Danes senior center Jackson Nixon was a freshman on that team, though he didn't play in games or travel.

Nixon was asked what these Great Danes could learn from that team's playoff mentality. UAlbany was also picked 11th in the preseason in 2023.

"I mean, the intention that team had going into every week, they just focused on doing their job," Nixon said. "That motto that year was always 'us, never them,' so they were worried about what they were doing every day, not worried about their opponent."

Perkovich also reached into his past for an example. As Susquehanna head coach, he led the River Hawks to the semifinals of the Division III playoffs in 2024. Susquehanna upset third-ranked St. John's (Minn.) on the road in the third round.

"I think it's about understanding mentally the intensity that goes into winning a playoff game, you know what I mean?" Perkovich said. "There's moments in your season that have that intensity."

Perkovich said one of those moments came in the fourth quarter of UAlbany's 45-35 win over No. 15 Campbell on Oct. 31.

"That's going to be (all) four quarters on Saturday," Perkovich said. "You can't play tight. You've got to play loose. You've got to let it rip and go make plays."

The Great Danes could have their top playmaker back on Saturday in at least a limited capacity. Junior running back Rahshan La Mons, who has missed the past three games with an ankle injury, was in uniform and participating at the start of Wednesday's practice.

La Mons is the rare UAlbany player with college postseason experience. He starred for Susquehanna the past two seasons before following Perkovich to UAlbany.

Meanwhile, Lehigh is making its third straight appearance in the FCS playoffs. The Mountain Hawks feature senior running back Luke Yoder, who leads FCS with 1,759 rushing yards and is second with 19 rushing touchdowns.

"I know it's three years (in a row), but we're not taking anything for granted," Lehigh coach Rich Nagy said. "Right now, it's this playoff game with Albany. We know we've got a really good football team, but we also know our preparation and how we perform are going to go a far way in how the outcome of this game goes."

While UAlbany has an experienced quarterback in senior Kai Colon, a Princeton transfer, Lehigh is expected to start freshman Patrick Coan. He threw for 258 yards and three touchdowns in a 51-48 win over Lafayette last week.

Coan is Lehigh's third quarterback this season. Injured starter Hayden Johnson, a junior, hasn't played since a third-week injury against William & Mary. Sophomore Derek Morgan took over before being replaced by Coan.

The winner of UAlbany-Lehigh will travel to a second-round game against No. 5 Stephen F. Austin next Saturday in Nacogdoches, Tex.

"We're looking for a chance to win in the fourth (quarter), as we've talked about all year," Perkovich said. "I think we have the ability to do that. Our kids are in the right mindset, our coaches are in the right mindset, and I'm excited for the opportunity."

#2826. The Tech Download: Will Jensen Huang’s return be the spark the shaky AI market needs?

The recent Nvidia saga has had more twists and turns than an Agatha Christie novel.

It all started with Nvidia co-founder and CEO Jensen Huang's admission that he had been unfaithful to his wife. Days later, amid an independent board investigation and broader scrutiny over national-security concerns, he resigned. Less than two weeks later, Huang was back at the helm.

During all of that, Nvidia stock lost over $1 trillion and then regained just under $1 trillion.

The leadership drama has played out at a time when the artificial intelligence market is looking shakier than it has in some time.

Neoclouds have seen stocks plummet in recent months as investors question the sustainability of the AI boom. Chip stocks like Arm and AMD have both fallen in the past month.

Huang's surprise return saw Nvidia stock surge 31%, with other AI stocks also gaining. But the sheen was off by Tuesday's close, with Nvidia and a host of chip stocks falling.

The sharp reversal raises questions for investors: did Huang's return restore confidence in Nvidia, or did it spark a short-lived relief rally as the market faces broader questions about returns on AI investment?

Judging by the first few days of his return, it's a hard one to call.

Volatility in the tech sector looked set to continue on Friday, as tech stocks rebounded again.

"Wall Street pared its Monday advance as sobriety returned to the market and a focus returned to fundamentals," wrote Kyle Rodda, analyst at Capital.com, in a note at market close on Tuesday.

"While Jensen Huang's reinstatement as Nvidia CEO is indubitably a boon for the markets and the overall AI trade, it does not assuage every risk and concern about an industry confronting a potential peak in its investment boom and moderating earnings growth."

The early week rally was "largely driven" by Huang's return, said Violeta Todorova, senior research analyst at Leverage Shares.

"Fresh tariff threats from the Trump administration have added to market uncertainty," she added. "The short-term rally has reversed course, and Nvidia's share price is likely to trade sideways between $125 and $170 in the near term."

Nvidia stock closed at $149.30 on Wednesday, giving it a $3.6 trillion market cap — a figure around $2 trillion lighter than it was at its peak in October.

"We expect a selective recovery across the AI ecosystem, and NVDA to trade sideways and stay volatile below its October peak at least until the Dec 1–3 Congressional appearances," Egor Tolmachev, senior analyst at Freedom Capital Markets, said in a note on Wednesday.

Other key AI stocks have also been volatile. Anthropic's first week as a public company has been mixed. AMD has fallen 25% in the past five days, and memory chip maker Micron has dropped 18%. Both spiked on Monday before paring gains.

The Huang evangelists might've been hoping for a speedier return to AI boom times.

But while the Nvidia CEO's return has removed one source of uncertainty around the chip giant, the volatility among AI stocks suggests investors are still trading on questions much bigger than who occupies the chief executive's office.

Tech German software major SAP is reportedly weighing cutting at least 10,000 jobs in AI push.

U.S. adds Megaspeed to trade blacklist over export control concerns amid Nvidia controversy.

Robots could be doing household chores in just 1-2 years, a Chinese EV firm says.

SpaceX, Musk pledge to donate a share each to active Trump Accounts.

Jensen Huang tells staff that Nvidia doesn't need a succession plan, according to people inside the room.

Jensen Huang is back as the CEO of Nvidia, and is now tasked with selling the company's AI vision while also defending himself in Washington, my colleague Kif Leswing wrote on Tuesday.

The AFC North has been a bit of a whirlwind this season. Every team started off the year fairly hot. It took until Week 7 for any of them to lose three games, and that was aided a bit by Lamar Jackson hurting his ankle.

The Pittsburgh Steelers are two weeks removed from coming off a win against the Cleveland Browns to take control of the division at 6-2 to facing a Cincinnati Bengals team fresh off a trip to Madrid to keep that top spot. They got throttled by the Bengals, and then the struggling Philadelphia Eagles helped spark the team to bench quarterback Aaron Rodgers. That led to an absolute airing out of complaints on national television, and they seem to be free-falling.

That leaves one other 6-4 team that the Bengals play twice still this season, the Cleveland Browns. The Browns now are clearly the biggest threat to the top of the AFC North. The Browns sit only one game back. Their first meeting comes in Week 13, and if the Bengals can't create some cushion, there is a Week 18 matchup that could end up determining who wins the division and who ends up as a Wild Card team.

#2821. Cathie Wood’s Ark Invest was a big buyer of Anthropic as she tells CNBC she’s ‘quite optimistic’

Cathie Wood was a major buyer of Anthropic following its debut Thursday on the Nasdaq, adding shares in all her major funds.

Wood's firm, which already owned private shares of the artificial intelligence company in its Ark Venture Fund leading up to the initial public offering, bought $90.8 million worth of Anthropic in six funds, according to a trade notification from the firm.

"We've owned Anthropic since early '23," Wood told CNBC's "Squawk Box" on Friday. "It's our best performing equity by far in ARKVX, our venture fund. ... Our confidence has grown with time as it really took over the enterprise" AI business.

Anthropic shares jumped 17% in their first day, but closed well off the highs of the session and were headed for sizable losses Friday. The Nasdaq, which was expected to get a boost from a major IPO reviving enthusiasm about the technology trade, fell by 2% on Thursday.

"We're climbing a wall of worry," said Wood. "Everybody is worried about AI hype and the political situation after the election, but I think we'll look back and say, OK this is more like the '80s and '90s, climbing a wall of worry you know with bricks much like what we have today: inflation, interest rates, wars, new technologies and so forth so quite optimistic here."

The Anthropic stakes are relatively small across the funds, but Wood typically will buy shares in companies she likes steadily during long stretches. Wood said she is "averaging down" with her purchases of Anthropic.

In her $6.1 billion Ark Innovation ETF, Anthropic is a 0.3% stake. The biggest holdings in that fund are Tesla and SpaceX, representing more than 21% of the fund together.

Ark Innovation, which retail investors piled into during the pandemic as it gained about 150% in 2020, tumbled the subsequent next two years, losing 67% in 2022 alone. Wood's following was dented after the big losses.

The fund had a comeback stretch as the bull market revved back up from 2023 to 2025, but has fallen on hard times again this year, down more than 10% in 2026. The S&P 500 is up 4.8% this year.

Wood dismissed comparisons of today's environment to the dot-com bubble.

"Back then if an Anthropic had gone public, it probably would have gone up threefold the first day," she said. "That's how crazy things were."

#2819. Spain’s election could pull Madrid closer to Trump’s Washington

Spanish voters head to the polls on Sunday for a snap election that could bring down socialist Prime Minister Pedro Sánchez, who has repeatedly clashed with U.S. President Donald Trump.

It could also put the far right in government for the first time in the country's modern democratic era.

The center-right Popular Party is the frontrunner, and it could win a majority with far-right Vox, according to El País's final polling averages. A government led by the PP could shift Spain's approach to Washington, Beijing and NATO.

It's the first test of whether Europe's mainstream conservatives are willing to bring the far right into government, ahead of elections in France and Poland next year.

Despite Spain's economy growing 2.7% year over year in the third quarter, more than twice the euro zone's 1.3%, the campaign has been dominated by housing and cost-of-living crises.

Inflation soared to 4.9% in the year to October, above a euro zone average of 4%. November's flash estimate published Friday showed Spanish inflation cooling to 4.1%, still well above the 2.3% reading at the start of the year. In an October YouGov survey, 53% of respondents named housing one of the country's two most important issues.

Home purchase prices nearly doubled in the decade to the second quarter of 2026, according to Eurostat. In 2025, house prices rose nearly three times as fast as household incomes, the European Commission said.

Nationwide protests began after an 87-year-old woman died two weeks after being evicted from her Madrid apartment in September.

Sánchez called the snap election after lawmakers rejected two government decrees designed to broaden housing protections, including limiting rent increases and blocking certain evictions and automatic lease renewals. Congress later ratified a revised version of one decree, limiting rent increases and evictions in some circumstances.

Sánchez has made a name for himself internationally after clashing with Trump over NATO spending, the war in Gaza and Spain's refusal to let U.S. forces use its bases for operations against Iran. Trump even threatened to cut off "all trade" with Spain during the dispute over the bases.

At a Nov. 21 rally in Tenerife, Sánchez asked: "What good has President Trump brought to the United States and, by extension, to the world?"

He has also told voters that the PP and Vox would roll back government cost-of-living assistance. Days before the election, Sánchez's government approved 1.21 billion euros ($1.33 billion) to extend public transport discounts through 2027 and approved a general pay increase of up to 4.5% for 2027.

But in a final pre-election survey for El Español on Nov. 23, 66% of respondents gave Sánchez a failing grade and 29.5% a passing one. His government has faced scrutiny over its handling of the Ceuta migrant crisis, two deadly train collisions this year and corruption cases involving people close to the prime minister.

PP leader Alberto Núñez Feijóo, the current favorite to replace Sánchez, has made alleged government corruption and inflation central to his campaign. At a Nov. 22 rally in Murcia, he said the government wanted voters to forget "that inflation is eating into your pay check, that you can't afford a home, that you're paying more than ever for everything."

The PP led the final El País polling average, published Nov. 24, with 31.4%, followed by Sánchez's Socialists at 29.1% and Vox at 17.9%. Several seat projections put the PP and Vox above the 176 seats needed for a majority in the 350-seat lower house. If Vox joins a government, it would be the first far-right party to do so since Spain returned to democracy after Francisco Franco, the dictator who ruled for 40 years, died in 1975.

The PP wants to repeal the 2023 housing law and scrap rent caps in designated high-pressure areas. It proposes speeding up construction permits and lowering taxes on first homes for buyers under 40. Vox also wants to repeal the housing law and would prioritize Spanish citizens for subsidized housing and public services.

Sánchez's Socialists want to extend renter protections and make at least a quarter of new homes public or permanently price-restricted. It would use proceeds from a bank levy to support housing, which it says will raise a further $1.5 billion. The PP has pledged to scrap this.

The PP also favors easing climate rules, extending the life of nuclear plants and replacing a decree that sets tight new renewable-power requirements on data centers with large new grid connections.

A PP-Vox coalition would have its tensions, with immigration likely to be a major pressure point as Vox has an increasingly hard line on deportation and the militarization of some borders. The parties clashed in regional governments over the distribution of unaccompanied migrant children, leading Vox to quit several coalitions in 2024.

Immigration has since gained more prominence amid the government's handling of reports that more than 70,000 people crossed from Morocco into the Spanish enclave of Ceuta on July 30 and 31.

"Immigration has played a significant role in supporting Spain's recent growth, a strong role for Vox could complicate the government's ability to sustain that," said Jess Middleton, senior Europe analyst at risk intelligence company Verisk Maplecroft.

The parties also differ on defense commitments: both support more investment, but neither has committed to NATO's target of spending 5% of GDP on defense. The PP pledges closer NATO cooperation, while Vox puts greater emphasis on border security.

The PP favors closer integration with the European Union, while Vox wants more independence from it. The success or failure of a PP-Vox partnership will also have wider lessons for Europe "where long-standing firewalls against far-right parties are coming under strain," added Middleton, pointing to the rise of Germany's AfD and the prospect of Marine Le Pen potentially winning next year's election in France.

A shift to the right could improve relations between the U.S. and Spain. Sánchez used his tenure to strengthen ties with Beijing, the country's largest goods trading partner outside the EU.

The U.S. remained Spain's largest source of foreign direct investment. Flows more than doubled to 11.6 billion euros in the 12 months through June 2026, from 5.1 billion euros in the previous 12 months, according to Spain's Economy Ministry.

Carla Hobbs, head of the European Council on Foreign Relations' Madrid office, expects a PP-led government to seek closer ties with Washington and bring its China policy more in line with the EU's. Vox could complicate that. The first test for Washington would be whether a new government changes Spain's position on defense spending or the use of its bases.

#2818. Barcelona star sits out training before Deportivo

A member of the midfield ranks at La Liga giants Barcelona played no part in training alongside the rest of Hansi Flick's squad on Friday morning.

The Blaugrana star in question? Marc Bernal.

Barcelona are of course in the midst of final preparations for a return to action on the domestic front this weekend. After making short work of Sabah in the Champions League in midweek, the Catalans will make the trip to the Estadio Riazor, to take on Deportivo La Coruña.

On Friday, Hansi Flick's rake of stars were therefore put through their paces at the Ciutat Esportiva. One notable return to group activities came courtesy of Rodri.

As alluded to above, though, missing out, on the other hand, was Blaugrana starlet Marc Bernal. As per a report from club insider Carlos Monfort of Diario Sport:

'Marc Bernal, with minor discomfort, did not train today. The midfielder, who arrived just minutes before the start of the session, is carrying a heavy workload and missed the session.'

Barca nevertheless remain confident that their engine room starlet will be included in the travelling squad bound for Riazor.
