NFL markets measure different outcomes, from the winner to an individual player's statistics. Confirm the market and scoring period before comparing prices. These examples use hypothetical lines.

Moneylines back a team to win outright. At -150, a winning $15 cash wager earns $10 profit and returns the $15 stake.

Point spreads apply a scoring handicap. A team at -3.5 must win by four or more points. A three-point win at -3 generally produces a push, returning the stake.

Game totals concern the teams' combined points. Over 44.5 requires at least 45. Check whether the market covers the full game, a half or a quarter.

Team totals concern one team's points. A team total over 23.5 requires that team to score at least 24, regardless of its opponent's score.

Player props concern individual statistics, such as passing yards or receptions. Over 250.5 passing yards requires at least 251. Participation and injury rules affect settlement.

Futures concern outcomes decided later, such as a division winner or Super Bowl champion. Read which games count toward season win totals.

Same-game parlays combine selections from one matchup. Every active leg must win. The operator's rules determine how a voided selection affects the remaining ticket and odds.

Check withdrawal support before choosing a deposit method. The cashier should show available methods, minimum amounts and any verification steps. A wallet or card accepted for deposits may have different withdrawal support.

I'd compare the transfer estimate with the amount I actually plan to withdraw. A quick method offers little help if the balance falls below its minimum. Check whether the sportsbook or payment provider charges a fee.

Track sportsbook approval separately from payment delivery. An approved request may still need time to reach your bank or wallet. Retain the transaction reference and check whether the account shows a document request.

Separate withdrawable cash from promotional balances. Bonus bets, FanCash and Betr Bucks have different redemption and settlement rules. Use the cashier's available withdrawal amount when submitting a request.

A welcome offer can also require later cash wagers. Budget for those commitments before depositing. Avoid increasing the deposit solely to pursue the advertised maximum reward.

Confirm acceptance and follow-up steps. Check bet history for the accepted ticket. Use the promotions page to track later claims or qualifying wagers. For Betr Picks, follow the fantasy entry and token instructions.

Choose an outcome, such as a team winning, a game going over its total or a player reaching a yardage threshold. The bet slip shows the line, odds and potential return. The sportsbook settles the ticket under the rules for that market.

The minimum age depends on the jurisdiction and product. Many sportsbooks require customers to be 21 or older. Some markets allow sports betting at 18. Check both the sportsbook's age requirement and the terms of the promotion you plan to claim.

Do I have to pay taxes on sports betting winnings?

Yes. The IRS requires you to report gambling winnings even if you receive no Form W-2G. Keep records of your wagers and payouts for tax reporting.

What happens to my NFL player prop if a player gets injured?

Check the market's participation and injury rules. At DraftKings, Early Exit Protected can void eligible full-game props when a player leaves injured in the first half and never returns. In an eligible parlay, DraftKings removes the voided leg and recalculates the odds on the remaining selections.

Verify the operator on your state regulator's licensed sportsbook list and download the official app. Check how to set deposit limits, take a break or self-exclude before funding an account. Wagers can lose even when the operator is licensed.

Decide how much you can afford to spend before opening the bet slip. Treat that amount as a limit and avoid increasing it to qualify for a promotion. If gambling is causing problems, support is available through the National Council on Problem Gambling.

#2816. New car prices are at a record high. Here's where to look for deals

Consumers who are in the market for a new car may find the latest pricing data somewhat sobering.

The average transaction price for a new vehicle reached an all-time high of $51,109 in October, up 2.1% from a year earlier, according to a report from Kelley Blue Book, an auto valuation company. The amount excludes incentives, taxes and fees.

But for consumers who can't spend that much — or don't want to — the good news is that plenty of buyers are shelling out much less for a vehicle.

"The average is telling us as much about product mix as it is about affordability," Erin Keating, executive analyst at Cox Automotive, which owns Kelley Blue Book, said in the report.

Of the five highest-volume car types sold in October, three — compact and subcompact SUVs, and compact cars — sold at prices far below the overall average transaction price, she said. For example, the average amount paid for a compact car in October was $28,030.

Additionally, sales of vehicles priced from about $28,000 to $38,000 climbed 9.6% from a year earlier.

"Consumers are still buying plenty of vehicles well below the headline average transaction price, even as strong demand for full-size pickups and other larger, well-equipped vehicles keeps pushing the average to new highs," Keating said.

The record price comes amid a 34% jump in the average cost of a new vehicle since October 2019, when it was $38,259, according to Kelley Blue Book data.

While pandemic-related supply shortages helped drive up prices earlier this decade, consumers' appetite for larger, more expensive vehicles like SUVs and pickup trucks has also pushed the average higher. Full-size pickups, for instance, sold for an average of $67,912 in October, according to Kelley Blue Book.

Yet buyers who would struggle to afford a $50,000-plus new car can still spend less and find deals in the current market.

For starters, automakers have been slower than usual to roll out 2027 models, at least in part because 2026 cars continue to sell well at current prices, said Ivan Drury, director of insights at Edmunds, an automotive research company. In October, 76% of new-vehicle sales were for current-year models, compared with 59% a year earlier.

This slowdown in the annual model-year transition means more 2027 cars will hit dealer lots soon, which suggests dealers and automakers are motivated to clear 2026 inventory, Drury said.

But waiting until the last week of the year to buy — thinking dealerships are desperate to make the sale — won't necessarily get you a better deal, he said.

"You're not going to get massive savings by waiting," Drury said. "Everybody's busiest time of the year is between Christmas and New Year's, but it's not like the best deals are then — that's a perception thing."

Some manufacturers are offering incentives on certain 2026 models, which can reduce the cost of a new car by thousands of dollars.

For example, the 2026 Nissan Pathfinder, a midsize SUV with a starting price of $41,565 — which includes destination charges — comes with a rebate of $5,000 for qualified buyers through Nov. 30, according to Cars.com, an online automotive marketplace.

Just be sure to check your local dealerships to confirm participation for any manufacturer's incentives, experts say.

Look for cars that have been sitting on dealer lots for an extended time, Drury said. In September, new cars took 58 days to sell, on average, according to Edmunds.

Generally, dealerships consider cars unsold for 60 to 90 days to be aging inventory, which may motivate them to offer a better deal, according to Autotrader.com, an online auto marketplace owned by Cox Automotive.

"You'll find the vehicles that dealers definitely want to get rid of the most," Drury said. Looking for older inventory will be "your quickest way to find a dealership that's thinking, 'Yes, I too want to get rid of this. Help me help you,'" he said.

Listings on some car-shopping websites include how long a car has been on a dealership's lot. You can also ask the dealership how long a particular car has been in its inventory.

Leasing may present an opportunity to get into a car with lower monthly payments than financing the same vehicle, depending on the terms of the lease.

However, fewer attractive leasing deals are available, a trend that started during the pandemic, Drury said. Plus, leases, which are typically for three years, usually come with mileage limits and charges for excessive wear and tear.

Nevertheless, leasing may make financial sense for a consumer who "gets that two- or three-year itch for a new car," Drury said.

This is because after the first two or three years of making payments on a car loan — a time period when the steepest depreciation generally occurs — the car's value may be less than what is still owed on the loan, depending on the specific terms. This is known as being upside down or underwater, or having negative equity. In that case, the amount must either be paid off or added to the buyer's new loan.

"We see so many cars traded in that are 3 years old, and the customer is upside down by $7,000, $8,000 or $10,000 and they roll that into another loan," Drury said.

You also may be able to find a good deal on a used car that's not too old.

The average transaction price for a 3-year-old vehicle was $32,039 in the third quarter, down slightly from $32,461 in the second quarter but 3.1% higher than $31,067 a year earlier, according to Edmunds. It's also higher than the average $27,133 list price for used cars of any age in October, according to Cox.

However, these 3-year-old cars are selling for far less than they did when they were new — $16,562 below the original sticker price of $48,601, on average. That marks the largest dollar gap Edmunds has on record, surpassing the previous high of $16,252 in the last quarter of 2019.

Some 3-year-old models tend to take longer to sell than others, which may give buyers some negotiating power, according to Edmunds. For example, a 3-year-old Jeep Cherokee, with an average transaction price of $23,138 — compared with its original price of $42,263 — takes an average of 64 days to sell, according to Edmunds.

Additionally, a good credit score can help keep the cost of financing a car purchase down. The higher your score, the lower the interest rate you can get, generally speaking.

And, finally, if you're trading in a car, be sure to shop it around, Drury said.

"Get multiple offers. They can vary by thousands of dollars," Drury said. "Have those offers in hand when you're at the dealership that has the next car you want to buy."

#2815. Embiid questionable, Brown out as Sixers visit Pacers in critical NBA Cup matchup

The Philadelphia 76ers are back in action Friday night after having the Thanksgiving holiday off, hitting the road to visit the Indiana Pacers in NBA Cup play.

Friday's contest will be the first leg of the team's fourth back-to-back of the season, with Philly set to visit the Houston Rockets Saturday night.

But first, the Friday matchup. Joel Embiid is questionable to face the Pacers due to illness. If he were downgraded to out, it would be his third game in a row missed due to being sick. Jaylen Brown is out due to left hamstring injury management. Brown exited the Sixers' Thursday win over the Miami Heat after limping to the locker room just before halftime due to hamstring tightness.

For Indiana, Tyrese Haliburton remains out as well as Ben Sheppard and Johnny Furphy. T.J. McConnell (hamstring), Kelly Oubre Jr. (hand) and Larry Nance Jr. (ankle) are all questionable.

The 6-12 Pacers have had a bumpy ride so far this season as they continue to deal with injuries up and down the lineup, most notably Haliburton being sidelined for all but the first four games of the year due to that hamstring injury.

Indiana most recently played all the way back on Monday evening, snagging a victory over Shai Gilgeous-Alexander and the Oklahoma City Thunder. Prior to that, however, they had lost four in a row. That's been the story of the season thus far for the Pacers: pick up a win, but lose the next bunch. They just haven't found a way to string victories together just yet.

This is the third meeting of the season already between the Sixers and the Pacers, with Philadelphia taking the wins in both previous contests, both by relatively narrow margins. Pascal Siakam led the way for Indiana in both of those games, posting a 26-point, 11-rebound double-double in one and a 31 points in the other. He's been leading the squad in general this campaign, posting 22.9 points per night on 50.7% field goal shooting and 37.0% shooting from long range.

Of the two games ahead of them in as many days, this game against the Pacers appears, on paper, to be the easier battle. I always add the caveat of that all just being "on paper" because, well, there are just no guarantees in the NBA. But that being said, Friday night is a good opportunity to add another game to the win column before a tougher matchup on Saturday night with some probably-weary legs.

It feels like a number of times the Sixers have trailed or lost this season has come down to their opponent being much hotter from long range. The Pacers, however, are second to worst in the association in three-point percentage (33.3%) so hopefully that means they won't bombard the Sixers with triples the way other teams have been able to at times. Hopefully.

If the Sixers can beat the Pacers Friday, they'll win the East's Group B and advance to the knockout stage for the first time since the NBA Cup was introduced in 2023-24.

The Sixers and Pacers tip off at 7:30 p.m. ET.

When: Friday, November 27, 7:30 p.m. ETWhere: Gainbridge Fieldhouse, Indianapolis, IN Watch: NBC Sports PhiladelphiaRadio: 97.5 The FanaticFollow: @LibertyBallers

#2814. EU leaders fail to break $1.7 trillion budget deadlock as political pressure mounts

European leaders failed to break a deadlock over the EU's next long-term budget on Thursday, leaving negotiators under pressure to meet their year-end target before a busy 2027 election cycle adds further complications.

National leaders are divided over the European Union's next long-term budget, which sets spending priorities and limits from 2028 to 2034. It requires unanimous approval from all 27 member states to pass, as well as a majority vote in favor from members of the European Parliament.

Representatives from EU member states met on Wednesday and Thursday to discuss the latest budget proposal from European Council President António Costa, which amounts to a spending plan worth 1.58 trillion euros ($1.73 trillion). It marks a downward revision of around 10% from the original proposals for the bloc's long-term budget — known as the Multiannual Financial Framework, or MFF.

The EU's budget negotiations come against a backdrop of surging government borrowing costs, spiraling inflation and sluggish growth across much of the continent, as well as concerns about rising budget deficits in various EU countries.

Failure to meet the year-end deadline for the budget to be agreed risks deepening the political deadlock at the center of the ongoing talks.

At a summit in Brussels Thursday, budget talks went on for 12 hours, according to a BBC report — with some participants said to have walked out of the meeting as conflicts emerged around spending priorities and aid for Ukraine.

A spokesperson for the European Commission was not available to comment on Thursday's talks.

German Chancellor Friedrich Merz told reporters on Thursday evening that the talks had brought "new movement" towards reducing the overall size of the budget, adding that leaders would reconvene in mid-December to resume talks.

But he also said: "The European Union comprises of 27 member states, not 23 or 24."

Merz, alongside the leaders of Denmark, the Netherlands, Austria, Finland and Sweden, said in September that the original 2-trillion-euro proposal would need to be reduced by "several hundred billion euros."

"We must make choices," they said in a letter addressed to Costa and Irish Taoiseach Micheál Martin, pointing out that their six countries finance almost 40% of all contributions to the budget.

They called for funding to be focused on defense spending, competitiveness and innovation and "the fight against irregular migration."

Costa labeled the revised budget plans "realistic" when he presented them last week, noting that despite the 10.2% reduction in spending the proposals still represented a 120% jump in competitiveness funding and a 232% upswing for defense compared to the current budget.

"We didn't reach an agreement," he said in a statement after Thursday's meeting. "Regarding the global volume of the budget, some member states asked for further reductions, most opposed further cuts. And some even proposed to reverse some cuts."

Some European parliamentarians also appeared unconvinced by the new proposals.

"Parliament is consistent and proposes more investments, [the] Council is inconsistent and proposes cuts," Romanian MEP Siegfried Mureşan said on Wednesday.

"When the Council finally reaches a position, that's when negotiations will start, not when they end. As prime ministers cannot dictate to national parliaments, prime ministers also cannot dictate to the European Parliament to adopt a budget that is too small, and which does not equip Europe for the future."

Elections are set to go ahead in key EU economies including Spain, Poland and Italy over the next 12 months, while France's spring presidential election is at the forefront of regional leaders' minds.

Current front-runner for the French presidency, far-right candidate Marine Le Pen, has previously called for the country to leave the EU and to cut its contributions to the bloc's budget.

Incumbent French President Emmanuel Macron stressed on Thursday that he had limited time left to have his say on EU spending.

"After 31 December, I will no longer be in a position, politically or democratically, to reopen discussions on the budget and bring them to a conclusion, as we will be ​entering a period far too close to the presidential election in France," he told reporters after the talks, according to French publication France 24.

France, the EU's second-biggest economy after Germany, is being tasked with drastically cutting spending to bring its 5.1% national deficit closer to an EU guideline of 3%, while reeling from months of mass protests, riots and strikes over education funding and a cost-of-living crisis.

#2813. Will BYU produce a complete game against Cincinnati? It better

Curtains will fall on the regular season for BYU and Utah this week.

The No. 2 Utes (11-0, 8-0) will host West Virginia. No. 10 BYU (9-2, 6-2) concludes its ride against Cincinnati at LaVell Edwards Stadium.

Utah is headed for the Big 12 title game and the College Football Playoff. BYU should be there, but it has had a season that has made it hard on itself.

Take the sentiments of CBS Sports analyst Adam Breneman. All season long, Breneman has praised BYU, Bear Bachmeier and the Cougar defense. He has been part of a national pundit wave that has backed coach Kalani Sitake.

That interest has been fueled by BYU's Notre Dame win, the "College GameDay" experience, social media influencers, and a remarkable BYU viewership of 32 million on TV (compared with Texas Tech's 17.4 million).

The CFP committee has continued to put BYU in the top 10 all four weeks.

Breneman's BYU review on YouTube is an anthem for folks in Provo — on and off the team — who know the situation very well.

Breneman says BYU can beat any team in the country — and lose to any team in the country; its next loss will be to itself.

At the top of his list of concerns is quarterback inconsistency. He does not mention that Bear Bachmeier has had to shoulder far too much of a load while star running back LJ Martin, the defending Big 12 Offensive Player of the Year, has been injured, which is a real factor.

"Bear Bachmeier does not need to be Superman. He does need to stop being the primary reason BYU loses," said the former college and NFL player.

"Before the Notre Dame game, Bachmeier completed 66% of his passes with eight touchdowns, one interception and a 5-0 record. Since then, the numbers have dropped to 58% completion, six interceptions and a 3-2 mark," he says. "The home-road split is even more concerning: 11 touchdowns and two picks at 66% at home versus three touchdowns, six interceptions and 56% on the road. BYU's likely first playoff game will be on the road.

"The tape shows the same pattern — four interceptions against Utah, a costly pick against Notre Dame, a fumble against Kansas and a 62-yard outing against TCU. BYU will go as far as Bachmeier takes it. Critical mistakes at this volume are no longer sustainable," Breneman said.

He does not note that BYU has had defensive stars Isaiah Glasker and Evan Johnson out with injuries.

Says the pundit, who has been in BYU's corner all season: "Perhaps the most alarming statistic on the roster is the missed-tackle rate. BYU ranks 135th out of 138 FBS teams, missing roughly one of every five tackle attempts. For a program with national-championship aspirations, that figure is untenable.

"Against UCF, the defense recorded 18 missed tackles and allowed a third-and-17 conversion because of them. Against Utah, a missed opportunity to sack Devon Dampier on third down was followed two plays later by the touchdown that gave the Utes the lead for good. Scheme and play-calling are irrelevant if the ball carrier is not brought to the ground. Against the best teams in the country, those misses will cost entire seasons, not just first downs."

In losses at Utah and UCF, two or three missed tackles loomed large in the outcome.

"No other serious playoff contender is giving away points and possessions at this rate. At Kansas, BYU missed a potential game-winning 37-yard field goal," he explained. "Against Notre Dame, BYU muffed a punt that produced three points and failed a fake punt try that produced three more. Against Iowa State, BYU muffed two punts that both turned into touchdowns. At Utah, the Cougars shanked a punt. Against Arizona State, BYU took a fair catch at its own 4-yard line.

"This is not a one-year issue. Last season's Big 12 championship game featured a failed fake punt, a muffed punt and a missed field goal. The cumulative points and field position surrendered this year are significant. Weeks of preparation on offense and defense are nullified when free possessions are handed over on special teams. National champions do not beat themselves this often."

Next, Breneman says coach Kalani Sitake has to display better game management and make better decisions.

"Kalani Sitake is one of the best coaches in the country. That does not exempt recent decisions from scrutiny. The pattern has been aggressive in moments that called for caution and conservative in moments that required aggression.

"In the Notre Dame game, leading 21-13 in the third quarter, Sitake called a fake punt from his own 28-yard line. It failed, Notre Dame scored a field goal, and Sitake later admitted he had been too aggressive.

"Against UCF, tied at 24 with roughly a minute left and facing third-and-6, BYU ran the ball instead of putting it in Bachmeier's hands or getting the ball to its playmakers. Iowa State went 0-for-3 on fourth-and-short situations, including two attempts inside the 5. Against Utah, a fourth-and-1 from the 7 was preceded by a delay-of-game penalty that forced a field goal.

"Head coaches are paid to judge which risks are worth taking. In the playoff, one misfired decision ends a season. The balance has been missing."

Breneman says all this is not a talent problem. Physicality and skill are visible across the roster. The problem is self-sabotage. When BYU has played its best football this season, it has looked like a team that can beat anyone. Right now it is making the kinds of mistakes that allow anyone to beat it.

"Bachmeier must protect the football," Breneman said. "The defense must tackle. Special teams must stop donating possessions. Sitake must manage the game with more consistent judgment. If those four areas are corrected, the national-championship ceiling remains real. If they are not, the team that ends BYU's season will not be Georgia, Ohio State or Indiana. It will be BYU."

BYU rolls. Utah easily takes care of business. Utah State will have its hands full.

#2812. Even the price of toys is on the rise. How Americans are scaling back holiday plans

As Amanda Brecht planned how her family would spend Thanksgiving this year, she had to make some difficult decisions.

Because of high gas prices, the 30-year-old decided she wouldn't drive across her home state of Michigan to celebrate with extended family. Instead, Brecht and her husband will spend the holiday with just their three kids. And with food prices rising, they'll be skipping appetizers and serving turkey breast instead of a whole bird.

"Thanksgiving is definitely going to look different this year," Brecht said. "Money doesn't grow on trees. You can only do so much."

With inflation accelerating, Americans like Brecht will see higher prices almost everywhere they look this holiday season. At a time of year synonymous with food, gifts and big spending, many consumers are instead pinching pennies, paring back their plans and making tough choices about how they'll celebrate.

Some are raiding their savings accounts and running up debt on credit cards. They're reporting downbeat outlooks about the economy in surveys. And they've become more selective with spending as inflationary pressures mount.

"Families are facing some tough choices this year," said Alexander Smith, an associate professor who researches behavioral economics at the Worcester Polytechnic Institute. "They might have to cut back on holiday spending in whatever way that they can."

A classic Thanksgiving feast will be harder to pull off as grocery prices rise.

The price of a 10-person Thanksgiving dinner increased 6% to $58.63 in 2026, according to an annual survey from the American Farm Bureau Federation. Faith Parum, the organization's deputy chief economist, said the price increase was a result of higher costs for labor and transportation.

Grocers including Walmart and Aldi have gained traction on social media by offering Thanksgiving meal bundles as a value-focused workaround to higher prices. Walmart's offering promises to feed 10 for $35, and includes a turkey and ingredients to make mashed potatoes, green bean casserole, cranberries, marshmallow-topped sweet potatoes and a pumpkin pie.

Still, some consumers will feel disappointed by having less choice in their menus due to higher prices, according to Joe Bruseulas, chief economist at tax firm RSM.

"We're missing the point here if all we're doing is focusing on the fact that Walmart will sell you a homogeneous dinner meal" at a discount, Bruseulas said. "People feel horrible that they have to go to that because the price of everything has just risen so significantly over the past five years."

Inflation is affecting more than Thanksgiving staples like turkey or stuffing. Food prices on average have jumped around 24% over the last five years, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics. Some items have seen even sharper increases: Coffee and ground beef, for example, have each climbed by more than 40% in the same period despite pulling back from recent highs.

At the same time, millions of Americans lost SNAP benefits under policy changes included in President Donald Trump's "big beautiful bill." Food banks across the U.S. have seen foot traffic skyrocket this year as the reduction in federal nutrition aid coincides with rising prices in grocery aisles.

The Salvation Army said some of its food pantries in October provided double or triple the amount of groceries than they did in the same month a year ago. Demand is now so high that some food banks in Feeding America's national network are worried about running out of supplies.

"We want to make sure that our food banks are as full as they possibly can be going into the holiday season," said Linda Nageotte, Feeding America's president and operations chief. "Far too many of them have empty shelves."

High energy prices as a result of the war with Iran are also affecting Americans' holiday plans.

Slightly fewer Americans will travel at least 50 miles around the Thanksgiving holiday in 2026, marking the first year-over-year decline since the country was in lockdown from the pandemic in 2020, according to AAA. Stacey Barber, a vice president at the auto association, attributed this year's pullback in part to higher fuel costs.

AAA said that about nine out of every 10 Thanksgiving travelers will do so by car. While gas prices have come down from a May peak, they are still up about 18% year over year and could set a new record for the month of November, according to the organization's data.

Travelers are paying about 31% and 23% more for roundtrip domestic airfare around Thanksgiving and Christmas, respectively, than they did a year ago, according to booking platform Hopper. Airlines have blamed the ticket price hikes on rising jet fuel costs in the wake of the war.

"There will be a significant shell shock when they see the price of traveling," said Hayley Berg, lead economist at Hopper.

Berg said price increases would feel more noticeable on routes previously serviced by Spirit, the budget airline that shut down in May. Travelers are booking holiday flights earlier than they did in past years, a sign that prices are top of mind, she said.

Staying home for the holidays isn't a silver-bullet solution, either, as heating costs jump.

The average American household is expected to spend 7% more on home heating this winter than they did last year, according to the National Energy Assistance Directors Association. For the typical family, that equates to an increase of about $66 and a total bill for the season above $1,000.

To be sure, some families will feel it more than others. Heating oil, which is used mainly in homes in the Northeast, has jumped around 70% year over year. However, natural gas, which is one of the most common heating types in the U.S., costs about the same as last year.

Some U.S. states, like Massachusetts and New York, beefed up their home energy support programs to account for higher prices. But Mark Wolfe, NEADA's executive director, said federal aid offerings haven't been meaningfully expanded this year.

"Some of these increases are caused directly by the decision to go to war," Wolfe said. "Why isn't the federal government helping families pay these higher bills?"

The spirit of giving will take on a different meaning for consumers this year.

Retail sales during the all-important holiday season are expected to grow almost 5% this year and surpass $1 trillion for the first time, according to Bain & Company. But the consulting firm said most of that growth would come from inflation, rather than from consumers buying more products.

And Citi on Wednesday said that increased holiday spending would be mainly driven by higher earners this year. The bank said that's a result of the "K"-shaped economy, a term used to describe a scenario where better-off Americans splash out while others struggle.

Toy prices have climbed more than 9% over the past year and now sit at their highest levels since 2019, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics. Manufacturers have raised prices in response to higher costs for transportation and plastic, according to Chris Byrne, an independent toy industry analyst. The artificial intelligence boom has driven up prices for the chips that power gaming devices and other electronics.

Byrne said that's causing shoppers to focus more on the quality of gifts rather than quantity.

"The toy industry always reflects the culture at large," Byrne said. "It's almost true economically as well. As inflation is rising, people are being a little bit more cautious about what they're buying."

Just 16% of respondents to a Morning Consult survey said they planned to spend more in the 2026 holiday season than they did a year ago. Respondents who were older or had lower earnings were more likely to say their budgets were shrinking, the survey found.

Some are doing away with gifts altogether. A poll from consulting firm TSG and the Electronic Transactions Association found 5% of Americans did not plan to buy any presents in 2026, up three percentage points from last year.

One shopper, Wendy Cooper, set a limit of one gift for each of her three adult children this year. For her teenage daughter, the 56-year-old Atlanta resident has scoured Five Below, the trendy value retailer, for budget-friendly presents.

"It's super stressful," Cooper said. "You have to weigh: Do you guys want lights on and a place to live? Or do you want gifts?"

Across the board, the Bureau of Labor Statistics' consumer price index reaccelerated after the U.S. first struck Iran in late February. Annual inflation rates have begun rising faster than wages in the same period, leaving consumers with less purchasing power.

Even if Americans keep spending for the holidays, economists caution that high prices during this busy shopping period can further drag on their economic outlooks. In the CNBC Voter Poll early this month, about three-fourths of participants rated the economy as poor or not good. Consumer confidence gauges had dropped significantly this year, but have regained ground in the runup to the holiday shopping season.

From her home in Northern Michigan, Brecht is doing everything she can to make Christmas feel special without breaking the bank.

The graduate student has been delivering DoorDash orders in her spare time and listing clothes on resale platforms to save up money for her kids' holiday gifts. Brecht is watching closely for discounts and will look for potential presents at thrift stores in the coming weeks.

If necessary, Brecht said she would turn to credit cards for some holiday purchases. She isn't alone: TSG and ETA's survey found that about one-third of Americans said they expected to go into debt during the holiday season, a roughly 74% increase from 2025.

"I don't really know how we're going to do it," Brecht said. "I'm just praying for good Black Friday deals."

#2811. Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double

Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers stayed unbeaten Saturday night at Xfinity Mobile Arena, earning a 127-116 win over the Bucks and improving to 3-0 on the season.

Joel Embiid had his 10th career regular-season triple-double, tallying 28 points, 11 rebounds and 10 assists.

On his 30th birthday, Jaylen Brown posted 27 points and five assists. Tyrese Maxey had 26 points. VJ Edgecombe scored 17 and grabbed 13 boards.

The Sixers' absences on the first night of a back-to-back included LeBron James (low back/sciatica management). Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said pregame that he did not know yet who would be available for his team's matchup with the Pistons on Sunday.

Here are observations on the Sixers' victory Saturday:

Dean Wade made his Sixers debut and started in James' place. He'd missed the first two games of the regular season with a left ankle sprain.

The 3-and-D forward scored the night's first points by a sinking a corner three-pointer on the opening possession.

Embiid also had a sharp start. He moved well, made good decisions and served as the main hub of his team's offense in the early going.

The Sixers' seven-time All-Star center has often enjoyed facing Myles Turner. He entered the night with an average of 29.4 points in 18 previous games against the Bucks big man.

Embiid did a little bit of everything in the first quarter, posting eight points, four assists and four rebounds over nine minutes. After snatching an offensive board, Embiid drained a fadeaway jumper to give the Sixers a 10-1 lead.

One of Embiid's assists was quite stylish. He found Edgecombe open beyond the arc with a behind-the-back bounce pass. Edgecombe knocked down the shot and wound up going 3 for 4 from three-point range in the first quarter.

The Sixers added rookie Labaron Philon Jr. to their rotation. Jabari Walker stayed in the mix, though he didn't enter until the 7:36 mark of the second quarter.

Philon played four minutes in his NBA debut and missed his lone shot attempt, a corner three. On defense, Philon allowed a couple of blow-bys off the dribble. He generally looked like he belonged on offense, albeit without any production in his brief run.

Maxey banked in a half-court heave at the first-quarter buzzer to put the Sixers up nine points. The Sixers couldn't carry forward any momentum, however, and began the second quarter poorly.

Adem Bona had another rough stint amid a subpar start to the season. After Anfernee Simons missed a transition layup try, Ryan Rollins' three lifted the Bucks into a 40-37 lead.

The dreadful stretch for the Sixers on both sides of the ball called to mind how they'd fallen into a 29-point deficit Thursday night vs. the Cavaliers before clawing out a comeback win.

The Sixers mustered just seven points in first six minutes of the second quarter and had many stagnant possessions that concluded with contested jumpers. The team's transition defense was dismal for much of the second period. Milwaukee had the game's first 19 fast-break points.

The Sixers regained the lead late in the second quarter. Kentavious Caldwell-Pope made two three-balls in the second and nabbed three first-half steals. Embiid assisted a go-ahead, and-one Walker layup.

Brown hit a three with 11.8 seconds left in the second quarter. Unsurprisingly, his variety of shotmaking skills were useful without James in the lineup.

Even when James or Embiid sit, the Sixers should have a simple advantage in terms of volume of stars over most opponents this year. None of Brown, Embiid or Maxey had the sort of off night that would have improved the Bucks' odds of a road win. They'd all passed the 20-point mark by the end of the third quarter.

Maxey notched another final-possession bucket, this time with a more conventional make. He canned a jumper from the right elbow with 1.7 seconds left in the third to extend the Sixers' lead to 97-87.

The Bucks made a late push in the fourth quarter. Kel'el Ware's put-back, and-one layup cut the Milwaukee's deficit to 117-114.

The Sixers ultimately handled business. Embiid reached his triple-double by assisting a second-chance Brown jumper and and Caldwell-Pope soon drilled a dagger corner three. Embiid even threw down a last-minute dunk for good measure.

For the second consecutive season, the Sixers have a 3-0 start. They got to four wins last year before dropping their first game.

#2810. Lindblad Says Las Vegas GP Felt Like Putting an F3 Engine in an F1 Car

Arvid Lindblad felt the Las Vegas race was artificial but cool, where he had to think like in junior formulas, driving on an F3 engine in an F1 car.

It was an emotional end to Racing Bulls driver Lindblad's race in Las Vegas from a Top 5 position due to a spin. He was in a good position but returned empty-handed, as did his team, with Liam Lawson starting a lap down due to a pre-race problem.

Lindblad kept himself in the hunt with the leading pack, as the slipstream factor became the crucial factor in the Grand Prix. He acknowledged the power unit of Red Bull Ford, but credited Mercedes with being a "rocket ship", considering how quick George Russell was.

Lindblad felt it was a shame how the racing is in F1 currently, which is decided early on. "In the end, F1 is the reality of the way the sport is at the moment; it's a bit of a shame that you don't get much racing," he said to the media at the F1 Qatar GP.

"90% of races are going to be won after turn two, which is a bit sad because it's an hour-and-a-half-long race, and after 30 seconds, who's going to win. And I think it was pretty cool for the sport to not know who's going to win until the last lap. For me, the reason is it's all low-speed tracks. There's no dirty air.

It's cold, and the way the tarmac is, the tires don't overheat." The biggest difference he could feel in Las Vegas was running half the lap on 500 horsepower rather than 1000. It felt like racing an F1 car using an F3 engine. He said that kind of thinking was last needed in the lower formulas.

Lando Norris leads Arvid Lindblad and Isack Hadjar in the Las Vegas GP. Image Credit: Manuel Eletto/Sutton Images / Getty Images / Red Bull Content Pool

"The biggest reason why it's different this year is that you do half the lap with 500 horsepower," continued Lindblad. "And you could say that's good or bad in the end. The engines are what they are this year, but I think it was one of the, I don't know if you'd say perks — but it actually worked well for racing.

"Because, in the end, in F1, you have 1,000 horsepower, and every other year they've gone to Vegas, you have 1,000 horsepower. You have the drag of an F1 car; you have 1,000 horsepower. Whereas in Vegas this year you do half the lap with 500 horsepower but with the drag of an F1 car.

"So, then it's like, it's like putting an F3 engine in an F1. You want the biggest tows you can find because it's super draggy, but you have no power. So then, that's why the tow was so powerful," summed up Lindblad, who found the racing cool eventually. He felt all the F1 drivers had to think about their moves and strategy.

It was not as plain sailing as it is in F1. The racing was more like in F3 or F4 or karting, where they had to think about when to stay in line and when to push around. Lindblad felt more alive being in a thoughtful Grand Prix, fighting for position rather than an automatic situation with a known result.

"I thought it was cool," said Lindblad. "I mean, just from a mentality point of view, the way I was having to think during that race, I haven't thought for, like, I mean, maybe in F3 I was doing a little bit. But it was kind of maybe F3 or F4, but honestly, karting. You think about when you're in a train, where do I want to be in the group?

"When do I want to be in the group? Where am I going to pass? All that matters is race time. It doesn't really matter about the position. You need to choose these thick conversations, these thoughts you're having, like when am I going to go? How am I going to go? You know, if the group's going to break away, I need to be in this position.

"You don't think about that now. What you think about now is lap one: get as far ahead as you can and manage the tires. It's quite a reality is most of the races relative to Vegas, it's somewhat boring. And yeah, it was just cool to be able to think that way. It's been a while," summed up Lindblad, who explained why it felt artificial.

Arvid Lindblad in the Las Vegas GP. Image Credit: James Sutton/LAT Images / Getty Images / Red Bull Content Pool

He reckons that had the deployment between the power units of Red Bull Ford, and Mercedes been similar, it would have been a more genuine fight. "In the end it is artificial," said Lindblad. "I think the thing that was sad about it was that the deployment made it look a bit silly.

"I think the fact that the RBPT and the Merc had very different deployment strategies made it look a bit silly because every lap George would pass Max and then Max would be passing him. If they're the same deployment, I think it would have looked pretty cool because then it would have been a genuine fight. So, I think it was great for the sport that there was racing. I think it's because of the engines that there was racing like this," summed up Lindblad.

#2809. As Anthropic shares tank, investors scramble to hedge. It could be very expensive

As Anthropic shares tumble toward their IPO price of $83, investors are looking for ways to hedge. Unfortunately, they may have to wait until Monday, when options on the AI giant are expected to list.

If Anthropic breaks its IPO price, it will represent a 27% decline from Thursday's intraday high of $113. The wild price swings offer clues on how expensive it might be to buy protection on the new tech giant once options begin trading.A 20% intraday move points to an implied volatility somewhere between 100 and 150 for Anthropic options, according to estimates from single-stock market-makers on the Cboe trading floor in Chicago.

Options traders are also looking at how SpaceX traded in the days following its offering for clues on how options might price.

Implied volatility in SpaceX started at 116, but that didn't deter options traders big and small from piling into the contracts tracking the stock, with about 1.7 million contracts traded on the first day for a total options premium of about $2.5 billion. Far out-of-the-money call contracts were some of the most popular trades as the stock soared 40% in its first three sessions.One key difference between now and then? Anthropic is trading against the backdrop of S&P 500 volatility close to 30, compared to 17.3 when SpaceX debuted. "I expect ANT options to be super active," said Tom Sosnoff, co-founder of the trading platforms ThinkOrSwim and TastyTrade. "I expect heavy options volume."One thing Anthropic and SpaceX have in common is heavy retail involvement in both IPOs, with retail brokerages receiving a 20% allocation from SpaceX and reports pointing to between a 15-20% allocation from Anthropic on Thursday. "Round one of the Anthropic IPO easily goes to the individual investor," Sosnoff added. "Retail participants bailed early at the best prices of the day while institutional investors paid up to top off their underwhelming allocations. I believe retail was aggressively reloading in the mid-90s."Further signs of retail demand will become evident when options likely begin trading on Monday. One thing to watch: the spread between out-of-the-money call and put contracts, which was heavily skewed toward calls in the case of SpaceX. "I would be careful comparing Anthropic to SpaceX," said Dennis Davitt, CIO and Co-Founder of Millbank Dartmoor Portsmouth. "From a market-structure perspective, the two are dramatically different. The availability and cost of borrowing will be different, the companies are at very different stages of maturity, and SpaceX has allowed employees to monetize portions of their holdings over time. All of those factors matter when pricing options."

#2808. Liesman: Why Greenspan could bet on productivity — and Warsh needs to hike

In a discussion on CNBC's Halftime Report Wednesday, the question came up: Why can't Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh hold the line on interest rates the way his icon Alan Greenspan did at the beginning of the dot-com era?

Greenspan famously leaned against inflation hawks on his committee in 1995 and 1996, holding the line on interest rates amid strong growth, because he believed in the productivity promise of the internet revolution.

It would be nice if Warsh could follow the same playbook amid the artificial intelligence build-out and keep rates steady until the productivity arrives. Unfortunately, a quick look at the numbers shows just how different the situation is for Warsh when it comes to inflation, the magnitude of the capital-investment boom and the monetary and fiscal stimulus boosting the economy.

Bottom line: the Fed looks like it has to hike — and maybe substantially — to be anywhere close to the position where the Fed was in Greenspan's time at the dawn of a major technological revolution.

It may be that the massive capex spending on AI will mean more productivity and less inflation down the road. But the front-end build-out is proving to be more inflationary, and more challenging, to a Fed already dealing with high inflation. In many ways, that's the opposite of what Greenspan had to handle.

#2802. Budget airline Norse Atlantic files for bankruptcy in Europe after failing to secure funding

Budget carrier Norse Atlantic filed for bankruptcy Friday, saying it failed to secure funding after soaring fuel costs strained the company's operations throughout the year.

Its parent company, Norse Atlantic ASA, concluded that the Norwegian carrier no longer has a basis to continue operations.

"Despite extensive efforts, the Company has so far not been able to secure sufficient funding to meet its short-term liquidity needs," the firm said in a statement Friday.

It comes days after Norse said it's facing a "critical financial situation" and was considering its options from a strategic takeover to ACMI contracts, which refer to short-term capacity leasing. The company's shares closed 85% lower on Wednesday.

The board has now filed a bankruptcy petition with the Agder District Court, where a trustee will be appointed to manage the process further.

"The Board of Norse Atlantic Airways AS has made the difficult decision to file for bankruptcy despite extensive efforts from the Company and the Group to find a solution," Norse Atlantic CEO Eivind Roald said.

"We are deeply sorry for the impact this has on our passengers, employees and partners. The Board, management and advisers of the listed entity Norse Atlantic ASA and the UK subsidiary are working to find a solution for the remaining part of the business to continue operations," Roald added.

The company has canceled all flights, and ticket sales have been discontinued.

Norse was founded in March 2021 and offered cheap flights between Europe, North America, Africa and Asia.

The Iran war began on Feb. 28 and caused jet fuel prices to skyrocket, forcing airlines around the world to grapple with higher costs. Prices are now up nearly 72% to $155 per barrel year-on-year as of the week ending Nov. 20, according to the International Air Transport Association's Jet Fuel Price Monitor.

Norse Atlantic's Roald told CNBC in May that he wasn't "overly concerned" about access to fuel but flagged that the company had canceled over 700 flights, which impacted more than 240,000 passengers.

Ryanair's CEO Michael O'Leary warned in April that if jet fuel prices continued to stay elevated throughout the year, then other European airlines were likely to face "financial difficulties" during the winter season.

In fact, several European airlines have stopped operating this winter, including Slovakia's AirExplore, Spain's AlbaStar, Ireland's Fly4 and Spain's Plus Ultra, which has canceled all flights but hasn't permanently closed.

#2801. AI shopping agents are having a moment, but Amazon and a growing number of sites threaten to stunt their growth

Maia Bittner urgently needed to replace her car's windshield wipers. So last month, she turned to Meta's Muse personal artificial intelligence agent.

Bittner, chief product officer at fintech startup Imprint, gave Muse the greenlight to buy some wipers on her behalf from AutoZone. Or so she thought.

About 17 hours later, she got a notification from her Muse agent, saying AutoZone canceled her order due to an unspecified error.

"Honestly, the most reliable path now is you ordering on Amazon yourself," Muse said, according to a screenshot of the message.

The interaction encapsulates the current messy and complicated landscape of agentic shopping apps, the AI tools that can make purchases on behalf of users. Over the past year, tech companies such as OpenAI, Meta, Google and Instinct have introduced versions of personal agents that promise to take the hassle out of browsing and buying online.

While some major retailers, such as Walmart and Sephora, have opened up their sites to agents, companies including Amazon, eBay, United Airlines and Kohl's limit what actions agents can take or block them outright. Others have added provisions to their terms of service spelling out that they're not liable if users' AI-driven purchases go wrong.

"It's a very mixed bag," Emily Pfeiffer, a principal analyst at Forrester, said in an interview. "It means that a consumer who's using an agent to shop and browse and learn is not going to see the full set of options because some websites are unavailable."

The hiccups threaten to stunt the growth of shopping agents by creating a clunky and frustrating experience for users who are just starting to experiment with the tools. The question of who takes the blame when a transaction goes wrong also remains unclear.

Despite those challenges, analysts remain bullish about agentic commerce's potential. U.S. revenue from agentic commerce could reach up to $1 trillion by 2030, analysts at McKinsey and ICSC forecast in April.

Retailers and tech companies are also positioning agents as a helping hand for holiday shopping. Walmart on Tuesday touted its partnership with Meta, saying consumers could "put an AI agent to work" to nab Black Friday deals.

Earlier this month, Shopify President Harley Finkelstein predicted this year would be the "first holiday season" where AI-driven shopping goes mainstream.

"I think last year we saw AI influence more in discovery," Finkelstein told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street." "I think now we're going to see more of that conversion."

For users hoping to turn their online shopping over to AI agents, the experience can be unpredictable.

Agents encounter a patchwork of permissions across various websites. Retailers such as Coach and Gap allow AI bots to browse but not check out, while others such as Target sometimes "block shopping agents by accident," according to an audit conducted in August by agent security company Billions Network.

Amazon has responded the most aggressively to agents by walling off its site entirely. The company rankled Elon Musk last month after it prevented his Grok Bot from placing orders.

In September, the company also blocked Meta's Muse from shopping on its platform, saying it never agreed to participate in the program. And last November, Amazon sued Perplexity, accusing the startup of concealing its Comet agent, which can purchase products on behalf of users.

Amazon CEO Andy Jassy has said the company expects "over time, to partner with third-party agents," but that any partnerships will require the right "value exchange" between agents and retailers.

Bots encounter issues even when retailers welcome them in. They often fumble through webpages and get stumped by login requirements at checkout. In some cases, the agents may require human intervention to bypass a Captcha test and continue browsing.

A Visa study published earlier this month instructed 22 models from 12 AI labs to carry out a range of shopping tasks, such as buying a TV, a laptop and a hair appliance. The agents failed about 43% of test runs, and in more than half of those failed runs, an agent purchased a product "that did not meet the customer's identified requirements," according to the study.

Bittner said she is a "big believer" in AI agents' potential. Earlier this year, she said, she was delighted when Perplexity's shopping agent found and purchased a pair of Adidas sneakers that were sold out for months.

Other experiences have been clumsier. Last week, she said, she asked Meta's Muse to help her find a Jellycat octopus plushie for her kids. It suggested a product marked 75% off from "Crazy Hobby Supply," a retailer she'd never heard of and that didn't appear to have any reviews, she said.

"I sort of trust Google to only surface sites that are not scams to me in their sponsored ads," Bittner said. "In the same way, I want AI to only surface to me websites that are not scams. I don't think we're there yet."

Users have reported mishaps with other AI tools, including one case where Instinct's text-based agent booked a hotel and signed up for a loyalty program without the consumer's consent.

Many retailers are approaching agents cautiously because they're concerned about fraudulent bot activity. San Francisco-based Skyfire is trying to solve that problem by arming retailers with more data to identify which bots are visiting their site and to verify that a human has actually consented to let an agent buy a product on their behalf.

Typically, when a human visits a website, there are "all kinds of signals" to prove it's not a fraudulent user, Skyfire CEO Amir Sarhangi said in an interview.

None of those things are available to retailers when an agent accesses their site, Sarhangi said. Agents might appear even more suspicious because they mask their IP address or origin via data centers, and they often use one-time, virtual cards to check out.

"So we talked to a lot of merchants and banks and financial institutions, and the things they told us were: We want to know who the end user is, and we want to know it's a real human," Sarhangi said. "We want to know who these agents are, because we're not going to just let willy-nilly anybody do these types of transactions, and we need a way to kind of verify that."

AI companies, retailers, financial institutions and other industries have announced an alphabet soup of "protocols," rules that propose ways to make it easier for agents to transact on websites.

There's yet to be a broad consensus around who takes responsibility when an agent purchase goes sideways. American Express in April said it would protect eligible customers "from charges related to AI agent error." Stripe, whose Link digital wallet is integrated with Muse, protects some agent purchases and offers a "refund guarantee."

Others are more wary. Last month, JPMorgan Chase blocked external agents from operating on behalf of customers, citing security concerns. Best Buy is among several retailers that deem users responsible for agent purchases in their terms of service. Best Buy says it views those transactions "as if you performed them yourself."

"Best Buy is not responsible for conduct, errors, actions, or inaction by any Digital Agent," the company's terms say.

Sarhangi said he can see why retailers, especially Amazon, are shutting their doors to external agents when the user experience remains inconsistent.

"At the scale [Amazon] operates in, I would think they need it to be much more aligned with their existing processes, so that they're not going to have a bunch of unhappy users saying, 'What's going on here?'" he said.

For now, agents remain a niche tool for shopping, with agentic purchases making up just 0.4% of global e-commerce transactions and 0.2% of value, according to Bain & Co. research published earlier this month.

Uber disclosed last month that AI agents booked only 504 rides and Uber Eats orders during its busiest week in October. Airbnb also said it has seen little volume from agents, while DoorDash said it has recorded only "several hundred" agent orders so far.

But retailers may not be able to ignore them forever. Roughly a quarter of Muse's 10 million users are shopping online via an AI agent with an average spend of $200 per order, Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg said in October.

Instinct CEO Noah Shinn told CNBC earlier this month that the average transaction value among its users exceeds $500 per person.

Asked about some companies' efforts to block agents, Shinn said he tries to "give them some slack."

"It's a new interface, it's a new mode of where users' attention is coming from, and it might just disrupt existing large services of theirs," Shinn said at a CNBC CEO Council dinner in San Francisco. "They just need time, and we've just done a lot of thinking on our side."

"We can obviously move very fast and certain businesses cannot," he said. "So, how can we collaborate with them?"

Gabriel Jesus will have to settle for a place on the bench as he waits for his opportunity to make an impact in the final quarter of the game if Barca are in need of a goal by then.

#2798. James can still take over ‘any given moment,' and Sixers trust he'll pick right spots

James can still take over 'any given moment,' and Sixers trust he'll pick right spots originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

LeBron James isn't oblivious to the calendar and the clock.

He knows he'll turn 42 years old next month, he's ancient by NBA standards and decline is inevitable.

James also knows who he is and what he still can do, whatever the norms are for players of a certain age.

"I think his battle against Father Time is one of the most impressive things he's done over the course of his entire career," Heat head coach Erik Spoelstra said Wednesday before Miami's loss to the Sixers. "We all know and everybody always says that Father Time always wins. But right now he's giving Father Time hell."

James went on to record his first triple-double with the 15-4 Sixers, posting 25 points, 12 assists and 10 rebounds. The four-time MVP is at 126 regular-season triple-doubles and counting in his career.

Only Russell Westbrook, Nikola Jokic, Oscar Robertson and Magic Johnson have more in NBA history. James is 17 above Philadelphia native and Sixers legend Wilt Chamberlain.

With Joel Embiid sidelined by illness for a second consecutive game and Jaylen Brown ruled out at halftime with left hamstring tightness, James' do-it-all performance was timely. Tyrese Maxey's 30-point, six-assist outing was essential, too.

"I'm just trying to play the game the right way," James said. "I don't think my job is to dominate the game every night for this ball club. We have a Finals MVP (Brown), we have a regular-season MVP (Embiid), we have an up-and-coming superstar at point guard, we've got a young fella in VJ (Edgecombe) who's up and coming as well.

"My dues are paid with what I've done. If they need me to dust off the cape from time to time, then I can still accomplish that. But that's not my job. … But if they need it, I can do it at any given moment."

James' night featured a flurry of very deep three-pointers in the second quarter.

He started the season 10 for 46 beyond the arc (21.7 percent), but his percentage has risen to 31.6 with two sweet-shooting games in a row.

"The work has been put in and I'm not ever worried about anything as far as my game," he said. "The difference between the start of the season and now is that my legs are feeling better, my ramp-up is feeling better.

"I'm about to be 42 years old in a month. S—, it takes a little bit longer for the ramp-up, but I've been feeling pretty good as of late. I don't ever second-guess myself … because I know the work that I put in."

While James' rebounding was perhaps the least spectacular aspect of his game, Sixers head coach Nick Nurse appreciated his work on the boards.

James has remained a statistically solid defensive rebounder into his 40s and he's one reason why the Sixers have made a major improvement in that department. According to Cleaning the Glass, the Sixers' opponents have grabbed 28.4 percent of their own misses, which ranks sixth in the NBA. The team ranked 27th last season.

Asked about James' cape comments, Maxey shouted out, "Dust it off then!" with a grin.

Maxey's not sure exactly when James will dramatically elevate his game, but he trusts the 22-time All-Star to pick the right spots.

"I don't know," Maxey said. "Early in the game, he was telling me he was tired. I said, 'Well, we don't have time for you to be tired.'

"But he does a good job of just managing the game. He knows when he needs to score, he knows when he needs to pass. We're going to need a lot from him. I know he's seasoned, but we expect a lot of him and he does that."

#2797. The S&P 500 is in a correction. Bear markets and recessions don't always follow

The S&P 500 fell into a correction on Nov. 20 for the first time since April 2025 as stocks pulled back sharply from October's record highs.

The market correction, defined as a 10% decline from a recent high, came as investor concern mounted over a potential slowdown in AI spending, mega-cap tech valuations and the path of Federal Reserve interest rate policy. The S&P 500 fell 3.7% on Nov. 20 alone, closing at 6,910.39, its seventh consecutive daily decline. That left the index 11.6% below the Oct. 6 closing high of 7,818.93.

A correction can deepen into a bear market, defined as a drop of 20% or more from the most recent top. But history shows that even a bear market, let alone a correction, doesn't automatically mean the economy is destined for a recession.

In a CNBC analysis, eight of the 13 S&P 500 bear markets overlapped with recessions, as defined by the National Bureau of Economic Research, and five did not. The NBER says a recession is "a significant decline in economic activity that is spread across the economy and lasts more than a few months."

The 1973-74 oil shock, the dot-com bust in 2000, the 2007-09 financial crisis and the 2020 pandemic downturn all overlapped with recessions. But some major bear markets also occurred without one, including in 1987 and 2022.

Deutsche Bank macro strategist Henry Allen said that every S&P 500 bear market since the 1962 "Kennedy Slide" has coincided with at least one of three factors: a recession, worsening economic data raising recession fears, or a cycle of Federal Reserve rate hikes. None of those are present today, at least so far.

"Much as last week's selloff was severe, it's worth remembering that it has several differences with many of the other big risk-off moves in recent times," the London-based strategist wrote on Tuesday.

Allen said there hasn't been any sudden reassessment of the growth outlook recently, which happened during the Covid-19 shock, when the pandemic pushed the global economy into recession and the S&P 500 plunged 33.9% in just 33 days. Nor is the current situation similar to the 2025 "Liberation Day" tariff period either, which also prompted a reassessment of the growth outlook.

Economic numbers aren't deteriorating either, with the November U.S. flash composite Purchasing Managers' Index, or PMI, at 54.6, above 50 and indicating that business activity continued to expand. Another factor that often coincides with a recession, Allen said, was a big hawkish shift from the central banks, seen in the 1966 and 2022 bear markets.

"Today however, the equity declines have not coincided with a hawkish repricing," despite widespread expectations that the central bank is set to raise rates again at its final meeting of the year, on Dec. 9, Allen said.

Today, markets are pricing fewer Fed rate hikes through September 2027 than they were on Oct. 6, when the S&P 500 set its record, Allen said. "If anything, pricing has shifted slightly dovishly with the equity declines."

Over more than six decades since 1962, bear markets have always coincided with at least one of those three circumstances, according to Allen.

"So it would be unusual to see one today without those" happening too, he said.

#2796. Trump demands probe into 'record setting error' after third-quarter GDP data gets massive revision higher

President Donald Trump on Thursday falsely deemed the latest gross domestic product revision a mistake, claiming that it should be "immediately investigated."

Data released Wednesday from the Commerce Department revealed that the U.S. economy exhibited much stronger growth for the third quarter than initially estimated, showing that real GDP grew at a 4.3% annualized rate. That was revised upward from the department's advance estimate of 3.4%.

That revision was a "RECORD SETTING ERROR," Trump said in a Truth Social post, suggesting that the initial release could have influenced the outcome of the 2026 U.S. midterm elections, where Democrats flipped both chambers of Congress and multiple governorships.

"I would like to wish a Happy Thanksgiving to even those who issued fake Economic Numbers, just prior to the Election, and corrected them yesterday, three weeks late, showing that the GDP was actually 4.3%, versus 3.4%, A RECORD SETTING ERROR, and one so grave that it should be immediately investigated," Trump wrote.

However, the difference between the advance estimate and the figure in the second release is far from being an all-time record. It was the largest revision since the 1.2 percentage point revision seen in the second quarter of 2020.

On an absolute basis, the latest revision was tied for 16th among all revisions from the advance estimate to the second release since 1990. It also tied four other quarters with a similar 0.9 percentage point difference — and was approximately double the average revision of 0.43 percentage points for all 147 quarters.

The upward revision shows Trump's policies are "delivering results" that defy expectations, White House spokesman Matthew Middleton told CNBC.

Revisions to U.S. economic numbers are not out of the ordinary. Initial reports count on partial survey data and early estimates. That data is later updated with more survey responses — which sometimes leads to changes from the initial reports.

GDP data is revised repeatedly over a five-year period. By the time the final revision is done, the average absolute error from the second release to the final is 1.14 percentage points.

The advance estimate for Q3 U.S. GDP, which came just days before Election Day in early November, was still better than the figure economists had expected at the time. It also showed a significant step-up in growth from the second quarter.

Because that was a strong showing for the economy, some on Wall Street consider this week's revision to be a confirmation of the economy's recent growth trend.

Jim Baird of Plante Moran Financial Advisors said immediately following the release that it "reinforces an increasingly familiar tension" that the economy is "performing quite well, but its resilience also gives the Fed an even stronger case for further rate hikes."

"The economy isn't merely hanging in there. It continues to demonstrate considerable resilience, with consumers spending, businesses investing and few signs that restrictive monetary policy is creating significant cracks in the broader expansion," the firm's chief investment officer said.

Traders are currently pricing in a nearly 100% chance of a hike at the Federal Open Market Committee's December meeting, according to the CME FedWatch tool. They're also anticipating an additional hike in January.

— CNBC's Jeff Cox and Fred Imbert contributed reporting.

Instead of the over-the-hill Rodgers, quarterback Mason Rudolph has been tasked with righting the ship.

This one could go one of two ways: The recent drama could serve to completely unravel the team, and usher in a move to can McCarthy before season's end. Or, McCarthy and the team could prove Rodgers and the court of public opinion wrong by using this nightmare as a chance to do big things.

I wouldn't expect Broncos head coach Sean Payton to oblige the ask. Denver is in a fight to win the AFC West, and victory today sends them to first place over the Chiefs. How do you see this one playing out?

With that, here's your open thread for Steelers vs. Broncos on Black Friday. As always, be kind to one another — and in all things… Go Bills!

#2793. Observations after James posts triple-double, Sixers beat Heat in NBA Cup

Observations after James posts triple-double, Sixers beat Heat in NBA Cup originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

LeBron James posted his first triple-double as a Sixer on Wednesday night and led his team to a 111-104 win over the Heat at Xfinity Mobile Arena.

James notched his 126th career regular-season triple-double with 25 points, 12 assists and 10 rebounds for the 15-4 Sixers.

Tyrese Maxey scored 30 points and dished out six assists.

Giannis Antetokounmpo recorded 32 points and 14 rebounds for the 10-7 Heat.

The Sixers improved to 2-1 and Miami fell to 2-1 in Eastern Conference Group B of the NBA Cup.

Jaylen Brown was ruled out at halftime with left hamstring tightness. He had six points on 2-for-8 shooting over 17 minutes.

"He'll get some imaging and we'll get the update tomorrow," Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said.

Joel Embiid (illness) missed his second game in a row. The Sixers are set to visit the Pacers on Friday night in their final Cup group game.

"He's ill," Nurse said of Embiid. "I do not know whether he's going to Indiana yet or not. I guess we'll find that out late tomorrow afternoon."

Here are observations on the Sixers' victory over the Heat:

Nurse emphasized pregame that the Sixers needed an improved defensive performance.

"We've got to compete much better at the defensive end than we did Monday (against the Trail Blazers)," he said. "It's as simple as that."

The night did not begin in especially promising fashion on that front. Antetokounmpo powered through Brown and scored inside for the game's first bucket. His transition layup put Miami up 6-0. The Heat scored nine fast-break points in the first six minutes.

As he had Monday in the Sixers' loss to Portland, Jabari Walker started at center. He was among the Sixers who shared the daunting defensive assignment of Antetokounmpo.

Walker took a charge on the 10-time All-Star in the first quarter, but Antetounkmpo had a stellar start. He forcefully orchestrated Miami's offense, often plowing through the middle of the floor and finding efficient answers.

The Sixers' defense was better late in the first quarter and it didn't hurt when Antetokounmpo took a seat.

Maxey had an aggressive, productive first period, scoring 13 points. Dominick Barlow's tip-in gave the Sixers a 27-26 edge.

James drained three three-pointers early in the second quarter, including a confident, deep jumper that fired up a high-volume home crowd. He's shown that he remains capable of flipping a world-class switch and taking over games in a vast array of ways.

There's no question the 22-time All-Star is out of his early-season outside shooting slump. James has gone 8 for 11 from long range over his last two games. He's scored at least 20 points in four straight.

"I just play the game as it unfolds," James said. "I had a couple of opportunities and I was able to connect on them. I kind of know when my hand is feeling pretty good, especially from the outside. We'll see how long I can keep it going."

Adem Bona checked in early in the second quarter. He provided a string of excellent defensive possessions against Antetokounmpo, including an emphatic block and several well-guarded missed jumpers, although Bona did get called for three fouls in the second period.

Nurse was pleased with what he received defensively from both Bona (two blocks, six rebounds) and Barlow (nine points, eight rebounds) off the bench.

"They used their gifts," he said. "They're quick side to side, both of them. When they're in that kind of fighting mode like that … they did a good job. We tried to get Bona in there when Giannis was in, just for the rim protection. And Dom just played really, really (well) all over the place … on the defense, on the rebounding, on the running, on the cutting."

The Sixers played effective zone defense at the tail end of the second quarter and held a nine-point halftime lead. That grew as high as 16 points in the third quarter.

Miami pulled back within single digits, but the Sixers maintained a healthy advantage and James kept on making highlight plays. He lobbed the ball up to Edgecombe early in the fourth quarter and the second-year guard skied high to lay it in. James later connected with Barlow on an alley-oop.

Once the Sixers have a substantial lead, James' presence is a massive help offensively. He's very well-accustomed to conducting methodical half-court offense, identifying favorable matchups and turning to fruitful actions over and over. James will also still steam forward on the fast break when he sees the chance.

Maxey buried a step-back three to extend his team's lead to 111-95 with 2:29 on the clock and the Sixers polished off a professional win. The fans roared when James snatched the rebound required to reach his triple-double.

"We came out tonight with a different mentality, different energy," Maxey said. "Nurse got on us a little bit in the film (session) yesterday. He lit a fire for a lot of us, which is good. We needed it."

#2788. The Nasdaq is in freefall. Here's one stock to look at

I asked Claude after Friday's close what I should do about the tanking stock market. Its advice was surprisingly useful: avoid panic selling and look for opportunities. A 40 VIX, which it touched Friday, might provide those opportunities. Specifically, a higher VIX (higher options premiums) signals fear, but it also creates an opportunity to sell elevated premium in some premium stocks, like Amazon.

Amazon's cloud business is growing at its fastest pace in nearly five years. AWS revenue rose 39.3% YoY in the third quarter, and management reported a $578 billion backlog. Trailing 12-month operating cash flow rose 29% to $168.3 billion. However, infrastructure investment accelerated, resulting in more than $42 billion in negative cash flow. The competing forces of accelerating growth and the cost of supplying it could keep the stock in its range over the next four weeks. Its shares are only off 14% from its recent highs, comparatively better than the Nasdaq Composite's near 19% decline.

AWS demand supports the lower end of the range. The backlog gives Amazon a basis for adding capacity even as investors scrutinize the cash cost. Cash spending could temper an upside rerating.

The next leg higher may require evidence that new capacity can earn attractive returns at this scale, and that evidence may be in short supply over the next couple of months.

Sell the Dec. 18, 2026 AMZN 235/285 strangle for an indicative $5.90 credit per share.

Max reward — $590 per strangle if AMZN finishes between $235 and $285 at expiration, before fees.

Breakevens at expiration — $229.10 and $290.90.

Max risk — Unlimited on the uncovered call, but one should think of the trade as risking getting short at a net price above $290, a nearly 13% premium to today's closing price. The downside loss would reach $22,910 per strangle if AMZN fell to zero. The credit is roughly 2.3% of a $260 reference share price over four weeks, or nearly 30% on a simple annualized basis. That comparison uses the share price as its denominator. Actual return on capital depends on margin and the cost to close or settle the trade.

Buying in bear markets, as the Nasdaq just touched, is never easy. But those opportunities don't present themselves too often. What are you going to do with it?
